<a id="sec:introduction"></a>
### Introduction

The density-functional variational viewpoint originates in the work of Hohenberg and Kohn <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[HK64](#ref-HK64)]. Uniqueness of a density–potential mapping and existence of a representing potential are separate questions; see the review <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[Penz23](#ref-Penz23)] for their precise distinctions. Kohn–Sham density functional theory seeks to reproduce the density of an interacting electronic ground state by noninteracting electrons in a common local potential <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[KS65](#ref-KS65)]. The constrained-search formulations of Levy and Lieb distinguish the existence of states with a prescribed density from the existence of a potential for which those states minimize the energy <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[Levy79](#ref-Levy79), [Lieb83](#ref-Lieb83)]. Allowing ensembles enlarges the latter class: several degenerate noninteracting ground states may be mixed to obtain a density that is unavailable from one determinant. Ullrich and Kohn make this distinction explicit in their treatment of singlet ground-state ensembles <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[UK01](#ref-UK01)]. Thus a failure of a particular orbital ansatz does not settle the ensemble representability question.

We consider the usual three-dimensional potential space $$\mathcal V=L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3;\mathbf R)+L^\infty(\mathbf R^3;\mathbf R).$$ For $v\in\mathcal V$, the noninteracting Hamiltonian $$H_s(v)=\sum_{i=1}^3\left(-\frac12\Delta_i+v(r_i)\right)$$ acts on the antisymmetric space $\bigwedge^3L^2(\mathbf R^3;\mathbf C^2)$, with the same scalar potential for both spin components. Its realization is determined by its semibounded closed quadratic form. A ground-state ensemble is a positive trace-class operator $\Gamma$ of trace one whose range is contained in the eigenspace at $\inf\sigma(H_s(v))$. We denote its spin-summed one-body density by $n_\Gamma$; it has integral three.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *Set $D=10^{200}$ and $e_z=(0,0,1)$. There is a finite positive integer $Z$, specified by [eq:explicit-charge](#eq:explicit-charge), for which the Coulomb Hamiltonian $$H_C=\sum_{i=1}^3
\left(-\frac12\Delta_i
-\frac{Z}{|r_i-(D/Z)e_z|}
-\frac{Z}{|r_i+(D/Z)e_z|}\right)
+\sum_{1\le i<j\le3}\frac1{|r_i-r_j|}$$ has a normalizable absolute ground state $\Psi$ in the sector $S_z=1/2$, normalized by $\|\Psi\|_2=1$, but there is no $v\in\mathcal V$ and no ground-state ensemble $\Gamma$ of $H_s(v)$ such that $$n_\Gamma=n_\Psi\qquad\text{almost everywhere on }\mathbf R^3.$$*

</div>

The two nuclei have positive integer charges and distinct finite positions. The theorem concerns a fixed finite system. The exact specification of $Z$ uses only spectral data, densities, and an angle minimization for a two-centre auxiliary problem; it does not test representability by other potentials. It is a nonnumerical specification, and no effective numerical bound for the charge is asserted.

shows that a density of a finite interacting Coulomb ground state can fail to have a noninteracting ground-state ensemble representation in $\mathcal V$. No restriction is placed on the degeneracy of a putative representing ground space or on the complex spinful states in its ensemble. The result makes no assertion about larger classes of distributional potentials, spin-dependent potentials, or nonlocal one-particle operators.

The same density also obstructs a local-potential first variation of the ensemble Hartree–exchange–correlation functional. More precisely, Corollary <a href="#cor:hxc-first-variation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="cor:hxc-first-variation">29</a> excludes a potential in $\mathcal V$ representing its one-sided variation along every segment in the common finite-energy density domain.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Relation to earlier work

Explicit prescribed densities can already fail noninteracting $v$-representability in $\mathcal V$. Wagner and collaborators discuss a positive two-electron density with finite kinetic energy whose interior cusp forces its inverse potential outside this class <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[WBSBW14](#ref-WBSBW14), Section IV.G, Eq. (36)]. Their example is specified directly, without a construction as a finite Coulomb ground-state density.

Trushin, Erhard, and Görling report numerical indications of failures of ground-state noninteracting representability from atomic density inversions <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[TEG24](#ref-TEG24)]. Open-shell inversion and symmetry restrictions are further investigated in <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ETG25](#ref-ETG25)]. The result here is an analytic exclusion for every ground-state ensemble of a specified finite Coulomb system, in the potential class $\mathcal V$.

Existence results for other settings must be distinguished from this question. The theorem of Chayes, Chayes, and Ruskai concerns quantum lattice systems with a fixed bounded kinetic-plus-interaction operator <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[CCR85](#ref-CCR85)]. Corso proves a rigorous Kohn–Sham formulation for spinless fermions on a bounded one-dimensional interval with generalized Neumann boundary conditions, allowing distributional potentials <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Corso26](#ref-Corso26)]. On a one-dimensional torus, Sutter and collaborators prove ensemble representability of strictly positive $H^1$ densities of prescribed particle number for spinful electrons, again allowing distributional potentials <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[Sutter24](#ref-Sutter24)]. These results do not supply a representability theorem for three-dimensional Coulomb densities in $\mathcal V$.

The asymptotic mechanism behind our construction was investigated by Gori-Giorgi, Gál, and Baerends <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[GGB](#ref-GGB)]. When the leading removal amplitude has a nodal plane, coupling to other ionic channels can give the density the same exponential decay rate on and off that plane, with an additional polynomial suppression on the plane. Their analysis identifies the role of the angular part of the Laplacian and the resulting difficulties for a regular Kohn–Sham potential. Gori-Giorgi and Baerends subsequently studied the effective equation for the square root of the density and exact harmonically confined examples <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[GGB18](#ref-GGB18)]. That effective equation is distinct from the common local potential determined below by two orbitals and an ensemble kinetic certificate. Here we prove the necessary estimates for a finite Coulomb system, and supply two further ingredients: a certificate valid against all ground-state ensembles and an obstruction to the entire sum space $\mathcal V$.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The mechanism and the proof

Scale length by $1/Z$. The nuclear charges become one, the half-separation becomes $D$, and the electron repulsion has coefficient $\lambda=1/Z$. The two lowest one-particle orbitals are a positive even orbital $b$ and an odd orbital $e$, positive above the median plane. Their splitting can be made arbitrarily small by increasing $D$. At zero coupling, the three-electron ground determinant occupies $b\uparrow,b\downarrow,e\uparrow$; its simple continuation in $S_z=1/2$ is the interacting state used below. Let $n$ denote the spin-summed density of this scaled interacting state.

Write $E_3,E_2$ for the three-electron and ionic ground energies, and put $$k=\sqrt{2(E_2-E_3)},\qquad A(r)=e^{-kr}r^\beta,
\qquad \beta=\frac{2-2\lambda}{k}-1.$$ The direct removal amplitude has a simple odd angular zero. In the complementary ionic space, the first nonzero planar term has amplitude of order $\lambda A(r)r^{-4}$. The full channel calculation proves that this coefficient is nonzero and controls the remainder uniformly through the region where it competes with the fast core term. Figure <a href="#fig:median-plane" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:median-plane">1</a> locates the plane and the translated windows used to compare these asymptotics with an orbital equation.

<figure id="fig:median-plane">

<figcaption>A meridional section of the scaled molecule, drawn schematically and not to scale. The nuclei lie at $± De_{z}$; the horizontal line is the median plane $z = 0$. A bounded window is translated to a distant point $pω$ in that plane. After normalization by $(A(p)/p)^{2}$, the limiting density in the window vanishes quadratically on the plane, although the actual density there has a smaller nonzero tail. This distinction drives the limiting-orbital and trace arguments.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-median-plane"></a>
**Figure source and visual witness** (`fig:median-plane`)

    node[midway,above] {$p\longrightarrow\infty$};

Visual witness description of PDF page 3 (generated from the PDF page, not source text):

```text
A schematic meridional cross-section diagram showing a vertical $z$-axis with nuclei marked by black dots at $+De_z$ and $-De_z$, and the origin labeled $0$. A horizontal line represents the median plane $z = 0$. A horizontal arrow pointing to the right along the median plane is labeled $p \longrightarrow \infty$. At a distant point $p\omega$ on the median plane, a dashed rectangular box labeled "translated window" is shown with local axes $z$ (vertical) and $\omega$ (horizontal). Beneath the translated window is the inequality $\sqrt{n(p, 0)} \gtrsim \lambda A(p)p^{-4}$.
```


Trigonometric parametrizations also underlie constrained kinetic-energy formulations for paired real orbitals <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[KR25](#ref-KR25), Section 3.3]. For the Coulomb density $n$, we minimize $$\int_{\mathbf R^3}n|\nabla\theta|^2,\qquad
\int_{\mathbf R^3}n\sin^2\theta=1,$$ over odd angles, folded into $[0,\pi/2]$ on the upper half-space. The orbitals $$g=\sqrt n\cos\theta,\qquad h=\sqrt n\sin\theta$$ have squared norms two and one, respectively. They obey common formal-potential equations with energies zero and $d>0$. A weighted spectral estimate proves that the paired/single determinant is a kinetic-energy minimum among *all* ensembles with density $n$. Consequently, any representing local potential must agree, up to a constant, with $V=\Delta g/(2g)$.

If $V\in\mathcal V$, translations of the orbital equations force every bounded limiting potential to equal $d+k^2/2$. This yields an exterior form bound and hence $$e^{ar}g\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)
\quad\text{for some }a>k.$$ The plane trace would then have the same exponential integrability. But $\theta=0$ on the plane, so $g=\sqrt n$ there, and the nonzero $\lambda A(r)r^{-4}$ tail makes that trace integral diverge. This is the contradiction.

Several arguments have uses beyond this example. Keeping the unperturbed fast channel controls the crossover without tracking every perturbed excited threshold. Spin orthogonality prevents cancellation and makes the relevant relative constants independent of the small level splitting. The angle construction gives an ensemble kinetic certificate from a weighted Poincaré inequality, even before the formal potential is known to belong to a standard potential class. Finally, translated weak potential limits, local form compactness, and an $H^1$ trace convert a plane asymptotic into an obstruction to $L^{3/2}+L^\infty$, without assuming pointwise convergence of the potential at infinity. We give the estimates and their hypotheses explicitly.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Organization and conventions

establishes the Coulomb spectral and perturbative facts. [sec:channels](#sec:channels) proves the uniform density asymptotics and their fixed-parameter counterpart. [sec:angle](#sec:angle) constructs the angle and proves the weighted gap. [sec:ensemble](#sec:ensemble) establishes the full ensemble certificate and identifies a hypothetical representing potential. [sec:obstruction](#sec:obstruction) proves the fixed-density obstruction () and the qualitative Coulomb family (). uses finite sufficient tests to select $Z$ and complete [thm:main](#thm:main). [sec:hxc-first-variation](#sec:hxc-first-variation) derives the functional consequence for the physical density.

All inner products are linear in the second variable. Norms of ionic channel vectors are Hilbert-space norms. Spatial reflection means reflection in the median plane; a simultaneous reflection is applied to all electron coordinates when discussing a many-electron state. Limits uniform for small coupling always keep $D$ fixed first. Later fixed-parameter limits keep the selected positive coupling fixed.

<a id="sec:setup"></a>
### The Coulomb system and its small-coupling limit

Let $$\mathfrak h=L^2(\mathbf R^3;\mathbf C^2),\qquad
 \mathfrak H_m=\bigwedge\nolimits^m\mathfrak h$$ be the one-electron and antisymmetric $m$-electron spaces. Spin is expressed in the basis $\uparrow,\downarrow$, and spatial reflections and rotations act trivially on spin. For $D>0$, put <a id="eq:unit-charge-potential"></a>$$u_D(x)=-\frac1{|x-De_z|}-\frac1{|x+De_z|},
 \qquad h_D=-\frac12\Delta+u_D,\qquad e_z=(0,0,1).
 \label{eq:unit-charge-potential}$$ The corresponding $m$-electron operators are <a id="eq:scaled-coulomb-hamiltonian"></a>$$H_{m,\lambda}
 =\sum_{i=1}^m h_D(x_i)
   +\lambda\sum_{1\le i<j\le m}\frac1{|x_i-x_j|},
 \qquad m=2,3,\quad \lambda\ge0 .
 \label{eq:scaled-coulomb-hamiltonian}$$ They act in $\mathfrak H_m$. Hardy’s inequality in a particle coordinate, or in a relative coordinate, gives $$\bigl\||x_i-R|^{-1}\psi\bigr\|_2+
 \bigl\||x_i-x_j|^{-1}\psi\bigr\|_2
 \le C\|\nabla\psi\|_2
 \le \varepsilon\|\Delta\psi\|_2+C_\varepsilon\|\psi\|_2 .$$ Thus these operators are self-adjoint on the antisymmetric part of $H^2(\mathbf R^{3m};\mathbf C^{2^m})$, and their form domain is the corresponding $H^1$ space. The same estimates show that $\lambda%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \KSOriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}
H_{m,\lambda}$ is an analytic family with this common operator domain. We write $E_m(\lambda)$ for its ground energy whenever the ground level under consideration exists.

For a normalized three-electron state, its spin-summed density is <a id="eq:density-definition"></a>$$n_\Psi(x)
 =3\sum_{\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\sigma_3}
   \int_{\mathbf R^6}
   |\Psi(x,\sigma_1,y_1,\sigma_2,y_2,\sigma_3)|^2\,dy_1\,dy_2 .
 \label{eq:density-definition}$$ In particular, $\int n_\Psi=3$. Ensemble densities are defined by linearity, as detailed in [sec:ensemble](#sec:ensemble).

<a id="stmt-prop-scaling"></a>
<div id="prop:scaling" class="proposition">

**Proposition 2** (Dilation to integer nuclear charges). *Suppose that the density of a ground state of $H_{3,1/Z}$, for a positive integer $Z$, has no noninteracting ground-state ensemble representation with a real spin-independent potential in $L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3)+L^\infty(\mathbf R^3)$. The physical Coulomb system with three electrons and charges $Z,Z$ at $$(0,0,D/Z),\qquad (0,0,-D/Z)$$ then has a ground-state density with the same obstruction.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-scaling"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-scaling</summary>

*Proof.* Use physical coordinates $\mathbf r_i$ and set $x_i=Z\mathbf r_i$. The unitary dilation $$(\mathcal U_Z\psi)(x_1,\ldots,x_m)
 =Z^{-3m/2}\psi(x_1/Z,\ldots,x_m/Z)$$ leaves spin unchanged. It transforms the physical Coulomb Hamiltonian into $Z^2H_{m,1/Z}$. In particular, normalizability, the ground-space property, and antisymmetry are preserved, and $$n_{\rm phys}(\mathbf r)=Z^3 n_{\rm scaled}(Z\mathbf r).$$ A physical noninteracting representing potential would transform into $$\widetilde v(x)=Z^{-2}v(x/Z).$$ For a decomposition $v=v_1+v_\infty$, with $v_1\in L^{3/2}$ and $v_\infty\in L^\infty$, the corresponding pieces satisfy $$\|Z^{-2}v_1(\,\cdot\,/Z)\|_{3/2}=\|v_1\|_{3/2},
 \qquad
 \|Z^{-2}v_\infty(\,\cdot\,/Z)\|_\infty
 =Z^{-2}\|v_\infty\|_\infty .$$ The dilation of the representing density matrix preserves its trace and support in the ground eigenspace. It would therefore give the excluded representation of the scaled density. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### The two lowest spatial orbitals

All subsequent one-particle orbitals are spatial orbitals. For fixed $D$ we abbreviate $u_D$ to $u$. Reflection symmetry will mean reflection in $z=0$, with $x=(x_1,x_2,z)$.

<a id="stmt-prop-two-centre-spectrum"></a>
<div id="prop:two-centre-spectrum" class="proposition">

**Proposition 3** (Two-centre spectrum). *For $D\ge100$, the two lowest eigenvalues of $h_D$ are simple. They have normalized real, axisymmetric eigenfunctions $b,e$, where $b>0$ is even and $e$ is odd and strictly positive on $z>0$. Write $$\epsilon_b=-\frac{\gamma^2}{2},\qquad
 \epsilon_e=-\frac{k_0^2}{2},\qquad
 d_*=\epsilon_e-\epsilon_b .$$ Then <a id="eq:two-centre-numerics"></a>$$0<d_*<\frac{100}{D},\qquad
 |\gamma-1|+|k_0-1|<\frac{200}{D}.
 \label{eq:two-centre-numerics}$$ The spectrum on $\{b,e\}^{\perp}$ lies more than $0.2$ above $\epsilon_e$. In particular, the spectral gap above $b$ in the even subspace is greater than $0.2$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-two-centre-spectrum"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-two-centre-spectrum</summary>

*Proof.* Let $\varphi(x)=\pi^{-1/2}e^{-|x|}$ be the hydrogenic ground state. We recall the two hydrogenic bounds needed for localization. After spherical-harmonic decomposition, the radial operators and first-order factors are $$H_\ell=-\frac12\frac{d^2}{d\rho^2}
       +\frac{\ell(\ell+1)}{2\rho^2}-\frac1\rho,
 \qquad
 A_\ell=\frac d{d\rho}-\frac{\ell+1}{\rho}+\frac1{\ell+1}.$$ Here $H_\ell$ has its Friedrichs realization in $L^2((0,\infty),d\rho)$, and $A_\ell$ is closed from $C_c^\infty(0,\infty)$. On the corresponding Friedrichs form domains, integration by parts gives $$\frac12A_\ell^*A_\ell=H_\ell+\frac1{2(\ell+1)^2},
 \qquad
 \frac12A_\ell A_\ell^*=H_{\ell+1}+\frac1{2(\ell+1)^2}.$$ The first identity and centrifugal comparison give $H_\ell\ge-1/8$ for $\ell\ge1$. Also $\ker A_0=\operatorname{span}\{\rho e^{-\rho}\}$, and the second identity gives $A_0A_0^*\ge3/4$. The polar decomposition transfers this last bound to $A_0^*A_0$ on $(\ker A_0)^\perp$. Thus the hydrogenic operator has ground energy $-1/2$ and is bounded below by $-1/8$ on $\varphi^\perp$; the radial $\ell=1$ function $\rho^2e^{-\rho/2}$ attains $-1/8$.

Choose a Lipschitz angle $\tau(\rho)$ that is zero for $\rho\le D/4$, is $\pi/2$ for $\rho\ge D/2$, and is linear between these radii. Its slope is $2\pi/D$. Around the two nuclei use cutoffs $\chi_\pm(x)=\cos\tau(|x\mp De_z|)$, set equal to zero outside their respective balls of radius $D/2$, and complete them by the nonnegative cutoff $\chi_{\rm out}$ to a quadratic partition: $$\chi_+^2+\chi_-^2+\chi_{\rm out}^2=1.$$ The two balls are disjoint. On either transition annulus the nonconstant pair is a cosine and a sine, so <a id="eq:two-centre-ims-cost"></a>$$\frac12\sum_{\nu\in\{+,-,{\rm out}\}}|\nabla\chi_\nu|^2
 \le \frac{2\pi^2}{D^2}<\frac{20}{D^2}.
 \label{eq:two-centre-ims-cost}$$ Lipschitz cutoffs suffice for the form identity; alternatively one may smooth the ramp with arbitrarily small increases in these constants.

On either inner ball, the attraction of the other nucleus has magnitude at most $2/(3D)<1/D$. On the support of $\chi_{\rm out}$, both nuclear distances are at least $D/4$; there the total attraction is at most $8/D$. Applying the IMS identity and the hydrogenic ground-energy bound gives <a id="eq:two-centre-bottom-lower"></a>$$h_D\ge-\frac12-\frac1D-\frac{20}{D^2}
 \quad\text{as a quadratic form}.
 \label{eq:two-centre-bottom-lower}$$ Here and below the exterior lower bound $-8/D$ is larger than the displayed inner lower bound for $D\ge100$.

If a test function $\psi$ is orthogonal to both $\chi_\pm\varphi(\,\cdot\,\mp De_z)$, then $\chi_\pm\psi$ is orthogonal to the ground state of the corresponding hydrogenic operator. The next hydrogenic energy is $-1/8$. Consequently, <a id="eq:two-centre-third-lower"></a>$$\langle\psi,h_D\psi\rangle
 \ge\left(-\frac18-\frac1D-\frac{20}{D^2}\right)\|\psi\|_2^2
 \label{eq:two-centre-third-lower}$$ on a subspace of codimension two.

For the upper bound, use the two disjointly supported trial functions $\chi_\pm\varphi(\,\cdot\,\mp De_z)$. Their squared norms exceed $1/2$, since the hydrogenic mass outside radius $D/4$ is $$e^{-D/2}\left(1+\frac D2+\frac{D^2}{8}\right)<\frac12.$$ The ground-state identity for the hydrogenic operator and the bound $|\nabla\chi_\pm|\le2\pi/D$ show that their Rayleigh quotients for $h_D$ are at most <a id="eq:two-centre-two-upper"></a>$$-\frac12+\frac{4\pi^2}{D^2}
 <-\frac12+\frac{40}{D^2}.
 \label{eq:two-centre-two-upper}$$ Their even and odd combinations give the same bound in the two parity subspaces. The Coulomb potential is relatively compact with respect to the free resolvent: one first truncates at infinity and near the nuclei, and uses local compactness for the bounded remainder. The omitted nuclear piece has operator norm from $H^2$ to $L^2$ of order $\sqrt{\varepsilon}$ when cut off to a ball of radius $\varepsilon$, by $H^2%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21AA}%
  \KSOriginalHookrightarrow\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}L^\infty$ and $\||x|^{-1}\mathbf 1_{|x|<\varepsilon}\|_2=O(\sqrt\varepsilon)$. The far-field remainder tends to zero in $L^\infty$. Hence the essential spectrum is $[0,\infty)$. The min–max principle now gives two negative levels, one in each parity, and no third level below the lower bound in [eq:two-centre-third-lower](#eq:two-centre-third-lower).

A full-space ground state can be chosen nonnegative by the absolute-value inequality for its kinetic energy. It is strictly positive, including at the nuclei. One direct way to include those points is to use $$b=\left(-\frac12\Delta+c\right)^{-1}
       (c+\epsilon_b-u)b,\qquad c>|\epsilon_b|,$$ whose resolvent kernel is strictly positive; the right-hand side is nonnegative and nonzero, and $b\in H^2(\mathbf R^3)$ is continuous. The same argument with the Dirichlet resolvent on $z>0$ gives strict positivity of its ground state in that half-space. Positivity and the usual ground-state simplicity argument identify the low even level with $b$ and the low odd level with the odd extension of that Dirichlet ground state. Simplicity also follows from the two-level count above. The full-space ground state cannot be odd, so $\epsilon_b<\epsilon_e$. Rotation about the $z$-axis preserves the unique normalized positive full-space and half-space ground states, proving axial symmetry.

Combining [eq:two-centre-bottom-lower](#eq:two-centre-bottom-lower), [eq:two-centre-two-upper](#eq:two-centre-two-upper) gives $$d_*\le\frac1D+\frac{60}{D^2}<\frac{100}{D}.$$ The same two bounds place $\gamma$ and $k_0$ within $200/D$ of $1$, with the stated bound on their sum. Finally, [eq:two-centre-third-lower](#eq:two-centre-third-lower), [eq:two-centre-two-upper](#eq:two-centre-two-upper) give the gap estimate $$\frac38-\frac1D-\frac{60}{D^2}>0.2 .$$ Since $e$ is odd, the same lower bound holds above $b$ in the even subspace. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Ground states and perturbation

<a id="stmt-prop-small-coupling"></a>
<div id="prop:small-coupling" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (Small repulsive coupling). *Fix $D\ge100$. For all sufficiently small $\lambda\ge0$, the ionic operator $H_{2,\lambda}$ has a simple isolated ground state, and $H_{3,\lambda}$ has a simple isolated ground state $\Psi_\lambda$ in the sector $S_z=1/2$. Its energy is an absolute ground energy of $H_{3,\lambda}$.*

*The ionic ground state is a spin singlet, is even under simultaneous reflection of its two spatial coordinates, and is axisymmetric. The state $\Psi_\lambda$ is real, axisymmetric, and odd under simultaneous reflection of its three spatial coordinates. The states and energies can be chosen analytic near zero; in particular, their state vectors converge with error $O(\lambda)$ in $H^2$. At zero coupling, <a id="eq:zero-coupling-states"></a>$$\Psi_0=b\uparrow\wedge b\downarrow\wedge e\uparrow,\qquad
 E_2(0)=2\epsilon_b,\qquad
 E_3(0)=2\epsilon_b+\epsilon_e .
 \label{eq:zero-coupling-states}$$ The ionic state has spatial factor $b(x_1)b(x_2)$ and the normalized singlet spin factor.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-small-coupling"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-small-coupling</summary>

*Proof.* At $\lambda=0$, the Pauli principle fills the spin orbitals in [eq:zero-coupling-states](#eq:zero-coupling-states). In the sector $S_z=1/2$, moving the doubly occupied spatial orbital from $b$ to $e$ costs $d_*>0$; using any further spatial orbital costs a positive gap by [prop:two-centre-spectrum](#prop:two-centre-spectrum). The displayed determinant is therefore a simple isolated ground state in that sector. The ionic ground state is likewise simple and isolated. The noninteracting ionization thresholds are $2\epsilon_b$ and $\epsilon_b$, respectively, and are strictly above these ground energies.

The common-domain analytic perturbation argument applies to each isolated rank-one spectral projection; see <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[Kato](#ref-Kato), Chapter VII, 1.3, Theorem 1.7, and 2.2, Theorem 2.6]. To include the graph-norm conclusion explicitly, write $V_{\rm ee}=\sum_{i<j}|x_i-x_j|^{-1}$ and $R_0(\zeta)=(H_{m,0}-\zeta)^{-1}$. On a contour enclosing only the relevant zero-coupling level, $$(H_{m,\lambda}-\zeta)^{-1}
 =R_0(\zeta)\bigl(I+\lambda V_{\rm ee}R_0(\zeta)\bigr)^{-1}.$$ Here $R_0(\zeta):L^2\to H^2$ and $V_{\rm ee}R_0(\zeta):L^2\to L^2$ are bounded uniformly on the contour. A Neumann series therefore makes the resolvent analytic as a map into $H^2$. Integrating around the contour, applying the resulting projection to the unperturbed state, and normalizing gives a real-analytic state in that space. Choose its real phase by positive overlap with the zero-coupling state. Spectral separation, or the min–max principle with the nonnegative repulsion, keeps this branch the lowest level of its sector.

It remains to check the absolute three-electron ground property. In the sectors $S_z=\pm3/2$, all three spatial orbitals must be distinct. Their zero-coupling energy is at least $\epsilon_b+\epsilon_e+\epsilon_3$, where $\epsilon_3=\inf\sigma(h_D|_{\{b,e\}^\perp})$; it exceeds $2\epsilon_b+\epsilon_e$ by more than $0.2$. Nonnegative repulsion preserves this lower bound, whereas the continued $S_z=1/2$ energy converges to $2\epsilon_b+\epsilon_e$. Spin flip identifies the two $S_z=\pm1/2$ ground energies. These four sectors exhaust the three-electron spin space, proving the claim.

All the stated symmetries commute with the Hamiltonian and its spectral projection. Their eigenvalues at zero coupling persist on the simple branch. For axial rotations, one may equivalently note that the projection of the invariant zero-coupling state is still invariant. The ionic singlet subspace is also invariant, so its spin character persists. ◻

</details>

For the rest of the small-coupling argument define <a id="eq:ionization-parameters"></a>$$k=k_\lambda=\sqrt{2\bigl(E_2(\lambda)-E_3(\lambda)\bigr)},\qquad
 A(r)=e^{-kr}r^\beta,\qquad
 \beta=\frac{2-2\lambda}{k}-1 .
 \label{eq:ionization-parameters}$$ Thus $k_\lambda\to k_0$. Once $D$ is fixed, the perturbation interval may be shortened so that <a id="eq:uniform-fast-slow-separation"></a>$$\gamma-k_\lambda\ge\frac12(\gamma-k_0)>0 .
 \label{eq:uniform-fast-slow-separation}$$ No lower bound uniform in $D$ on the length of this interval is asserted or needed.

<a id="stmt-lem-density-regularity"></a>
<div id="lem:density-regularity" class="lemma">

**Lemma 5** (Density regularity and compact convergence). *Regard $F_{\rm true}(x)=\sqrt3\,\Psi_\lambda(x,\cdot)$ as a Hilbert-space-valued function, retaining the removed-electron spin and all ionic coordinates and spins. Then $$F_{\rm true}\in H^2(\mathbf R^3;\mathcal K),\qquad
 n_\lambda=\|F_{\rm true}\|_{\mathcal K}^2
       \in H^2(\mathbf R^3)\cap L^1(\mathbf R^3),\qquad
 \sqrt{n_\lambda}\in H^1(\mathbf R^3),$$ where $\mathcal K$ is the corresponding fiber Hilbert space. Moreover $\Delta n_\lambda\in L^1(\mathbf R^3)$. These assertions hold for every $H^2$ three-electron eigenstate, with no small-coupling assumption.*

*For the branch in [prop:small-coupling](#prop:small-coupling), $n_\lambda\to n_0=2b^2+e^2$ in $H^2$ and $L^1$, hence uniformly on compact sets; the densities are commonly tight as $\lambda\downarrow0$. Each fixed compact set has a positive lower bound for $n_\lambda$ for sufficiently small $\lambda$. The density is even and axisymmetric.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-density-regularity"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-density-regularity</summary>

*Proof.* Fubini’s theorem applied to the derivatives in the distinguished coordinate gives the Hilbert-valued $H^2$ assertion. In three dimensions the corresponding Sobolev estimates yield $$\|F_{\rm true}\|_{L^\infty_x(\mathcal K)}
 +\|\nabla_xF_{\rm true}\|_{L^4_x(\mathcal K)}
 \le C\|F_{\rm true}\|_{H^2_x(\mathcal K)} .$$ The distributional product identities <a id="eq:density-product-identities"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 \partial_j n_\lambda
 &=2\operatorname{Re}
      \langle F_{\rm true},\partial_jF_{\rm true}\rangle_{\mathcal K},
 \notag\\
 \partial_i\partial_j n_\lambda
 &=2\operatorname{Re}
      \langle\partial_iF_{\rm true},\partial_jF_{\rm true}\rangle_{\mathcal K}
   +2\operatorname{Re}
      \langle F_{\rm true},\partial_i\partial_jF_{\rm true}\rangle_{\mathcal K}
 \label{eq:density-product-identities}
\end{aligned}$$ then prove $n_\lambda\in H^2$. The same identities and Cauchy–Schwarz, now using $L^2_x$ on both factors, give $\Delta n_\lambda\in L^1$. The norm chain rule gives <a id="eq:density-kinetic-bound"></a>$$\int_{\mathbf R^3}|\nabla\sqrt{n_\lambda}|^2
 \le \int_{\mathbf R^3}\|\nabla_xF_{\rm true}\|_{\mathcal K}^2 .
 \label{eq:density-kinetic-bound}$$

The product estimates also show continuity of $F%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \KSOriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}\|F\|_{\mathcal K}^2$ from $H^2_x(\mathcal K)$ to $H^2_x$. Thus the graph-norm convergence in [prop:small-coupling](#prop:small-coupling) gives the claimed density convergence. For the $L^1$ convergence, use $$\|n_\lambda-n_0\|_1
 \le
 \bigl(\|F_{{\rm true},\lambda}\|_2+\|F_{{\rm true},0}\|_2\bigr)
 \|F_{{\rm true},\lambda}-F_{{\rm true},0}\|_2 .$$ It implies common tightness. Since $b$ is continuous and strictly positive everywhere, $n_0\ge2b^2$ has a positive minimum on each compact set. Uniform convergence gives the corresponding compact lower bound for $n_\lambda$. The spatial symmetries follow by taking the squared fiber norm. ◻

</details>

The last lemma makes no claim of global positivity from compact convergence alone. The exterior argument below supplies positivity outside a fixed ball uniformly for sufficiently small coupling, and thereby supplies the global positivity needed for the angle construction.

<a id="sec:channels"></a>
### Exterior channels and the density near the median plane

The leading removal amplitude has a simple zero on the median plane, but a complementary ionic channel supplies a nonzero density tail there with the same exponential rate. This is the nodal-plane mechanism discussed in <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GGB](#ref-GGB), Section V.A]. We prove the estimates, including their uniformity as the interaction tends to zero, without assuming an asymptotic expansion of finitely many Dyson orbitals.

In the perturbative part of this section $D$ is fixed and sufficiently large, and $0\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_D$, where $\lambda_D>0$ may be decreased finitely many times. Exterior radii and constants denoted by $C_D$ may depend on $D$, but not on $\lambda$ in this interval. For $x=(x_1,x_2,z)$, write $r=|x|$, $p=(x_1^2+x_2^2)^{1/2}$, and $\omega=x/r$ when $r>0$. Differentiation in $p$ keeps $z$ fixed. Put <a id="eq:channel-radial-factor"></a>$$k=\sqrt{2(E_2(\lambda)-E_3(\lambda))},\qquad
 \beta=\frac{2-2\lambda}{k}-1,\qquad A(r)=e^{-kr}r^\beta.
 \label{eq:channel-radial-factor}$$ We can arrange that <a id="eq:channel-rate-separation"></a>$$k>0.99,\qquad \gamma-k\geq\tfrac12(\gamma-k_0)>0.
 \label{eq:channel-rate-separation}$$

<a id="stmt-prop-density-properties"></a>
<div id="prop:density-properties" class="proposition">

**Proposition 6** (Density estimates). *For all sufficiently large $D$ and sufficiently small $\lambda\geq0$, the density $n=n_\lambda$ is positive, even in $z$, and axisymmetric. It is smooth away from the nuclei and converges locally uniformly to $2b^2+e^2$ as $\lambda\downarrow0$.*

*Given $\eta>0$, first $D$ can be chosen sufficiently large and then $\lambda_D>0$ sufficiently small so that, beyond a radius independent of $0\leq\lambda\leq\lambda_D$, <a id="eq:density-drift"></a>$$|\partial_r\log n+2k|\leq\eta,\qquad
 \left|\partial_p\log n+2k\frac p r\right|\leq\eta
 \quad(0\leq z\leq p/2).
 \label{eq:density-drift}$$ With $L=10$ and $m(p,z)=e^{2kp}n(p,z)$, the same choices give <a id="eq:strip-data"></a>$$\frac{|m_p|+|m_{pp}|}{m}\leq\eta,\qquad
 \frac{n(p,z_1)}{n(p,z_2)}\leq20
 \quad(0\leq z_1\leq z_2\leq L)
 \label{eq:strip-data}$$ for all sufficiently large $p$, uniformly in that coupling interval.*

*For each fixed such $\lambda>0$, there are $c_\lambda>0$ and $P_\lambda<\infty$ such that <a id="eq:plane-tail"></a>$$\sqrt{n(p,0)}\geq c_\lambda A(p)p^{-4}\qquad(p\geq P_\lambda).
 \label{eq:plane-tail}$$ There is a number $a>0$, depending on $D,\lambda$, with the following translation properties. If $x_j=p_j\omega_j$ lies in the median plane, $p_j\to\infty$, and the horizontal unit vectors $\omega_j\to\omega_*$, then <a id="eq:plane-translate"></a>$$\frac{n(x_j+x)}{(A(p_j)/p_j)^2}
 \longrightarrow a^2z^2e^{-2k\omega_*\cdot x}
 \quad\hbox{locally uniformly for }x=(x_1,x_2,z)\in\mathbf R^3.
 \label{eq:plane-translate}$$ If $|x_j|\to\infty$, $|(x_j)_3|\to\infty$, and $x_j/|x_j|\to\omega_*$, then <a id="eq:bulk-translate"></a>$$\frac{n(x_j+x)}{n(x_j)}
 \longrightarrow e^{-2k\omega_*\cdot x}
 \quad\hbox{locally uniformly in }\mathbf R^3.
 \label{eq:bulk-translate}$$*

</div>

The quantitative uniformity needed for the final parameter prescription is given in [eq:quantitative-density-drift](#eq:quantitative-density-drift) below. In particular, the constants remaining after the exterior limit do not involve the inverse of the small splitting $d_*$.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### The fiber equation and an exterior cutoff

Let $\mathcal K$ be the Hilbert space of the antisymmetric ionic pair, including its spin coordinates and the distinguished electron’s spin, restricted to total spin projection $1/2$. Regard $$F_{\mathrm{true}}(x)=\sqrt3\,\Psi_\lambda(x,\cdot)$$ as a $\mathcal K$-valued function, with $\|F_{\mathrm{true}}(x)\|^2=n(x)$. The ionic operator acts trivially on the distinguished spin. Set $$B=H_{\mathrm{ion}}-E_2(\lambda),\qquad
 P=|\phi\rangle\langle\phi|,\qquad Q=1-P,\qquad B_Q=B|_{Q\mathcal K},$$ where $\phi$ is the normalized ionic singlet tensored with distinguished spin up. Only that distinguished spin is compatible with the singlet and total projection $1/2$; hence $P$ has rank one and <a id="eq:ionic-gap"></a>$$B\geq0,\qquad B_Q\geq c_D>0
 \label{eq:ionic-gap}$$ uniformly on the small coupling interval.

For normalized spin orbitals $v,w$, let $\Phi_{v,w}$ denote their normalized two-electron determinant. Expansion of the unperturbed determinant in the distinguished coordinate gives <a id="eq:unperturbed-fibers"></a>$$\begin{split}
 F_0(x)&=e(x)\phi_0+b(x)G,\\
 \phi_0&=\uparrow\otimes\Phi_{b\uparrow,b\downarrow},\\
 G&=\uparrow\otimes\Phi_{b\downarrow,e\uparrow}
       -\downarrow\otimes\Phi_{b\uparrow,e\uparrow}.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:unperturbed-fibers}$$ Consequently <a id="eq:core-spin-components"></a>$$\|G\|=\sqrt2,\qquad
 \|G_\uparrow\|=\|G_\downarrow\|=1,\qquad B_0G=d_*G.
 \label{eq:core-spin-components}$$ The ionic spatial parity of $G$ is odd, whereas that of $\phi$ is even. Thus $QG=G$ at every coupling under consideration.

<a id="stmt-lem-channel-cutoff"></a>
<div id="lem:channel-cutoff" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Cutoff channel equation). *There are an exterior $\mathcal K$-valued function $F$, a nonnegative bounded multiplication operator $W(x)$ on the ionic variables, and $\sigma>\gamma$ such that <a id="eq:cutoff-channel-equation"></a>$$\left(-\frac{\Delta_x}{2}+\frac{k^2}{2}+B+u(x)+\lambda W(x)\right)F=R.
 \label{eq:cutoff-channel-equation}$$ The operator $W$ equals $\sum_{i=1}^2|x-y_i|^{-1}$ for $|y|\leq r/2$, vanishes for $|y|\geq3r/4$, and satisfies <a id="eq:cutoff-interaction-symbol"></a>$$\|\partial_x^\nu W(x)\|_{\mathcal K\to\mathcal K}
 \leq C_\nu r^{-1-|\nu|}.
 \label{eq:cutoff-interaction-symbol}$$ Here $\nu$ is a Cartesian multiindex. For every fixed integer $s$, <a id="eq:channel-residual"></a>$$\|\partial_x^\nu R(x)\|\leq C_{D,s}\lambda e^{-\sigma r}
 \quad(|\nu|\leq s).
 \label{eq:channel-residual}$$ Moreover, <a id="eq:density-cutoff-error"></a>$$\left|\partial_x^\nu\{n(x)-\|F(x)\|^2\}\right|
 \leq C_D e^{-2\sigma r}\quad(|\nu|\leq2),
 \label{eq:density-cutoff-error}$$ and $F-F_0=O(\lambda)$ on each fixed exterior annulus, also in any fixed finite number of $x$-derivatives.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-channel-cutoff"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-channel-cutoff</summary>

*Proof.* Put $\rho=(|x|^2+|y|^2)^{1/2}$. A configuration-space partition according to a particle with distance at least a fixed multiple of $\rho$ from the origin gives the exterior form bound <a id="eq:configuration-exterior-bound"></a>$$H_{3,\lambda}\geq2\epsilon_b-o(1)
 \quad\hbox{on functions supported where }\rho\to\infty.
 \label{eq:configuration-exterior-bound}$$ The nuclear attraction of the selected particle is $O(1/\rho)$; the other two one-particle operators are each bounded below by $\epsilon_b$, and repulsion can be dropped. The partition has derivatives $O(1/\rho)$, so its kinetic localization error tends to zero. This argument is uniform for $0\leq\lambda\leq1$.

The exponent margin needed below already follows for $D\ge100$ from [eq:two-centre-bottom-lower](#eq:two-centre-bottom-lower), [eq:two-centre-two-upper](#eq:two-centre-two-upper): $$\begin{gathered}
 k_0^2\ge1-\frac{80}{D^2},\qquad
 \gamma^2\le1+\frac2D+\frac{40}{D^2},\\
 \frac{10}{9}k_0^2-\gamma^2
 \ge\frac19-\frac2D-\frac{1160}{9D^2}>0.
 \end{gathered}$$ We can therefore choose $$0<\tau_0<\tau_1<\tau_2<k_0,\qquad \tau_0\sqrt{10/9}>\gamma.$$ After decreasing $\lambda_D$, the exterior bound and $E_3(0)=2\epsilon_b-k_0^2/2$ leave a positive margin above the weight cost $\tau_2^2/2$. The exponential-weight identity, first with an exterior cutoff and a smooth truncated exponent whose gradient has norm at most one, gives <a id="eq:configuration-weight"></a>$$\|e^{\tau_1\rho}F_{\mathrm{true}}\|_{H^1(\mathbf R^9)}
 +\|e^{\tau_1\rho}F_0\|_{H^1(\mathbf R^9)}\leq C_D.
 \label{eq:configuration-weight}$$ At zero coupling the same argument also gives $\|e^{\tau_2\rho}F_0\|_{H^1}\leq C_D$, since $\tau_2<k_0$. The finite spin sum is understood. The estimate also applies to an inhomogeneous equation: if $(H_{3,\lambda}-E_3(\lambda))v=f$, then, for a fixed ball $K$, <a id="eq:inhomogeneous-weight"></a>$$\|e^{\tau_1\rho}v\|_{H^1}
 \leq C_D\left(\|e^{\tau_1\rho}f\|_2+\|v\|_{H^1(K)}\right).
 \label{eq:inhomogeneous-weight}$$ Indeed the exterior spectral lower bound controls the weighted $L^2$ norm by the weight identity and Cauchy–Schwarz. Combining a small multiple of the kinetic form estimate with that lower bound also controls the gradient; the strict margin between $\tau_1$ and $\tau_2$ absorbs its additional weight cost. Hardy’s inequality absorbs the infinitesimally form-bounded Coulomb terms. These bounds are independent of the exponent truncation, which can therefore be removed.

The equation for $v=F_{\mathrm{true}}-F_0$ has source $$\bigl(E_3(\lambda)-E_3(0)-\lambda V_{\mathrm{ee}}\bigr)F_0.$$ Hardy’s inequality, with the spare exponent $\tau_2$ on $F_0$, bounds it by $C_D\lambda$ in weighted $L^2$. The compact term is $O(\lambda)$ by [prop:small-coupling](#prop:small-coupling). Thus <a id="eq:weighted-state-difference"></a>$$\|e^{\tau_1\rho}(F_{\mathrm{true}}-F_0)\|_{H^1}\leq C_D\lambda.
 \label{eq:weighted-state-difference}$$

Choose smooth functions of $|y|/r$, with values in $[0,1]$, such that $\chi=1$ on $|y|\leq r/3$, $\chi=0$ on $|y|\geq r/2$, and $\zeta=1$ on $|y|\leq r/2$, $\zeta=0$ on $|y|\geq3r/4$. Define $$F=F_0+\chi(F_{\mathrm{true}}-F_0),\qquad
 W=\zeta\sum_{i=1}^2|x-y_i|^{-1}.$$ On the support of $\zeta$, $|x-y_i|\geq r/4$, proving [eq:cutoff-interaction-symbol](#eq:cutoff-interaction-symbol). Denote the operator on the left of [eq:cutoff-channel-equation](#eq:cutoff-channel-equation) by $\mathcal L_\lambda$. Since $W$ is exact on the support of $\chi$, <a id="eq:cutoff-residual-identity"></a>$$R=(1-\chi)\mathcal L_\lambda F_0
       +[\mathcal L_\lambda,\chi](F_{\mathrm{true}}-F_0),
 \label{eq:cutoff-residual-identity}$$ where $$\mathcal L_\lambda F_0
 =\left\{\lambda\left(|y_1-y_2|^{-1}+W\right)
          -E_3(\lambda)+E_3(0)\right\}F_0.$$ Thus every residual term is $O(\lambda)$ and is supported where $|y|\geq r/3$. There, <a id="eq:cutoff-geometric-margin"></a>$$\rho\geq\sqrt{10/9}\,r.
 \label{eq:cutoff-geometric-margin}$$

We give the derivative details, since higher ordinary derivatives of the uncut wavefunction across a collision cannot simply be iterated. Local $H^2$ estimates on nested fixed-size configuration boxes have uniform constants: the Laplacian estimate with cutoffs and the infinitesimal Coulomb bound absorb the potential terms; the local energy estimate controls cutoff commutators. Where derivatives of $\chi$ occur, $x$ is separated from both other electrons. Further $x$-derivatives of the equation differentiate only smooth bounded coefficients, while singular ionic coefficients are independent of $x$. Difference quotients followed by these local estimates give any fixed number of $x$-derivatives of the difference, at size $O(\lambda)$ in weighted local norms. In the other residual term, $F_0$ has the product form [eq:unperturbed-fibers](#eq:unperturbed-fibers); its $x$-derivatives have no electron-electron singularity, and the ionic Coulomb multiplier is controlled by Hardy’s inequality. Summing the box estimates and using Sobolev embedding in the three $x$-coordinates gives [eq:channel-residual](#eq:channel-residual). A small loss from $\tau_1$ toward $\tau_0$ absorbs polynomial integration factors. By [eq:cutoff-geometric-margin](#eq:cutoff-geometric-margin), one still has $\sigma>\gamma$.

Density derivatives in the discarded region require a different coordinate choice. Choose smooth pair cutoffs $\chi_{ij}\in[0,1]$ equal to one at distances at most $1$ and zero at distances at least $2$, and expand the product of the three identities $1=\chi_{ij}+(1-\chi_{ij})$. For each term use the connected components of its near-edge graph as clusters. They have diameter at most $4$, and distinct clusters are separated by at least $1$. Nested boxes in which each particle moves by less than $1/8$ preserve a positive separation. The cluster containing $x$ is far from the nuclei when $r$ is large. Translate with $x$ all other variables in that cluster. A derivative in the translated $x$-coordinate is the sum of the corresponding particle derivatives. It annihilates within-cluster Coulomb singularities and singularities involving only outside particles; every coefficient differentiated is smooth with uniformly bounded derivatives. Nested-box energy and elliptic estimates can therefore be iterated in these three translation directions. This is the parallel-derivative method of <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[FS18](#ref-FS18), preprint version, Proposition 1.10 and Section 6]; here the fixed separations make the constants uniform, while [eq:configuration-weight](#eq:configuration-weight), [eq:weighted-state-difference](#eq:weighted-state-difference) preserve the requisite exponential weights.

The difference $n-\|F\|^2$ is a sum of integrals of products of $F_{\mathrm{true}}$ and $F_0$, with coefficients supported where $|y|\geq r/3$. After the coordinate changes just described, differentiate these integrals and apply Cauchy–Schwarz to the weighted local estimates. Each product has two exponentially decaying factors. Sobolev embedding in the three translation directions and [eq:cutoff-geometric-margin](#eq:cutoff-geometric-margin) give [eq:density-cutoff-error](#eq:density-cutoff-error), after another arbitrarily small loss of exponent. To obtain smoothness on a compact set disjoint from the nuclei, replace the pair-cutoff radii $1,2$ by $\varepsilon,2\varepsilon$, with $\varepsilon$ smaller than one sixteenth of the set’s distance from the nuclei. The cluster containing $x$ has diameter at most $4\varepsilon$; sufficiently small nested boxes therefore keep it away from the nuclei and preserve the intercluster separation. The same argument without discarding a tail then proves smoothness of the density there. On fixed exterior annuli the differentiated local estimates also give the claimed $O(\lambda)$ convergence of $F-F_0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Localized multipoles and the slow channel

Reduced ionic inverses preserve the moment bounds needed below. Let $\rho_y=(1+|y|^2)^{1/2}$. For some $s_D>0$, $\phi$ is bounded uniformly in $e^{s_D\rho_y}H^2_y$. Moreover, if $e^{s\rho_y}v\in L^2$, then <a id="eq:weighted-ionic-inverse"></a>$$e^{s'\rho_y}B_Q^{-1}Qv\in H^2_y
 \quad\hbox{for some }0<s'<s,
 \label{eq:weighted-ionic-inverse}$$ with uniform bounds on the small coupling interval. To justify this, conjugate the ionic operator by a small exponential with smooth truncated exponent. The added first-order term has small relative operator bound and the added zeroth-order term is bounded and small. An isolated contour around the ground eigenvalue has uniformly bounded conjugated resolvents. Applying the resulting projection to a compactly supported vector with nonzero ground-state overlap proves localization of $\phi$. The operator $B+P$ has a bounded inverse into $H^2_y$. Its conjugated finite-rank term is now bounded by the localization of $\phi$, so the same inverse perturbation argument applies. Restriction to $Q$ proves [eq:weighted-ionic-inverse](#eq:weighted-ionic-inverse). Bounds are independent of the exponent truncation. Polynomial multiplication is harmless after decreasing the exponent again; only finitely many such decreases will be used.

On these localized vectors, finite Taylor expansion gives <a id="eq:ionic-multipoles"></a>$$W=\frac2r+\frac{W_2(\omega)}{r^2}
       +\frac{W_3(\omega)}{r^3}+\cdots,\qquad
 W_2(\omega)=\omega\cdot(y_1+y_2).
 \label{eq:ionic-multipoles}$$ An expansion through $r^{-m}$, applied to a vector bounded in one of the preceding weighted spaces, has remainder $O(r^{-m-1})$, with Cartesian derivative of order $|\nu|$ bounded by $O(r^{-m-1-|\nu|})$. On $|y|\leq3r/4$, the Taylor remainder is bounded by a constant times $|y|^m r^{-m-1}$. Outside this region the polynomial moments of the localized vector bound the omitted cutoff tail exponentially. Differentiation gives the same symbol bounds. These are expansions on localized vectors, not operator-norm expansions on all of $\mathcal K$. Similarly write $$u=-2/r+\sum_{j\geq2}U_j(\omega)r^{-j},\qquad
 U_2=0,\qquad U_3=-D^2(3\omega_z^2-1),$$ with finite-order Taylor remainders understood.

<a id="stmt-lem-channel-expansion"></a>
<div id="lem:channel-expansion" class="lemma">

**Lemma 8** (Slow-channel expansion). *Write $PF=\phi f$. With every fixed finite number of angular derivatives, <a id="eq:channel-leading-bounds"></a>$$\|F\|=O(A),\qquad \|QF\|=O(A/r^2).
 \label{eq:channel-leading-bounds}$$ There are smooth real odd scalar functions $a_0,a_1,a_2$ on $\mathbb S^2$ such that <a id="eq:projected-channel-expansion"></a>$$f=A\left(a_0+\frac{a_1}{r}+\frac{a_2}{r^2}+\mathcal E\right).
 \label{eq:projected-channel-expansion}$$ For each fixed angular differential operator $\Omega^\ell$, <a id="eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives"></a>$$\Omega^\ell\mathcal E=O(r^{-3}),\qquad
 \partial_r\Omega^\ell\mathcal E=O(r^{-4}),\qquad
 \partial_r^2\Omega^\ell\mathcal E=O(r^{-4}).
 \label{eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives}$$ The radial remainders are odd in $\omega_z$; in particular their bounds without angular differentiation gain a factor $|\omega_z|$, also after one or two radial derivatives. All bounds are uniform for small $\lambda$. Furthermore $a_0^\lambda\to a_0^0$ in $C^1(\mathbb S^2)$, and phases can be chosen so that <a id="eq:simple-angular-zero"></a>$$\inf_{\omega_z>0}\frac{a_0^\lambda(\omega)}{\omega_z}>c_D>0.
 \label{eq:simple-angular-zero}$$ Finally, <a id="eq:fast-orbital-expansion"></a>$$b=e^{-\gamma r}r^{2/\gamma-1}
       \bigl(b_\infty(\omega)+O(r^{-1})\bigr),\qquad
 \min_{\mathbb S^2}b_\infty>0,
 \label{eq:fast-orbital-expansion}$$ with angular derivatives and two radial derivatives of the expansion.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-channel-expansion"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-channel-expansion</summary>

*Proof.* The ionic gap and multipole estimate imply $$PW\phi=(2/r+O(r^{-2}))\phi,\qquad QW\phi=O(r^{-2}).$$ Splitting a vector into $P+Q$ and applying Young’s inequality to the off-diagonal term, while retaining half the positive $Q$-gap, gives <a id="eq:ionic-schur-bound"></a>$$B+\lambda W\geq 2\lambda/r-C_D/r^2.
 \label{eq:ionic-schur-bound}$$ The norm $N(x)=\|F(x)\|$ consequently satisfies $$\left(-\Delta_x/2+k^2/2-(2-2\lambda)/r-C_D/r^2\right)N\leq\|R\|.$$ Take the real inner product of the vector equation with $F/\|F\|$ and use $|\nabla\|F\||\leq\|\nabla F\|$. Replacing the norm by $(\|F\|^2+\varepsilon^2)^{1/2}$ proves the weak inequality also at zeros. The unbounded ionic operator is handled through its quadratic form, or by bounded spectral truncations.

Applying this scalar operator to $A(1-M/r)$, and dividing by $A$, gives $r^{-2}$ coefficient $$kM-\beta(\beta+1)/2-C_D.$$ Choose $M$ to make this positive, and start outside $r>2M$. A sufficiently large multiple dominates the inner boundary value and the exponentially smaller source, yielding $N=O(A)$ by exterior comparison. The latter can be proved by testing the positive part of the difference with cutoffs: the zeroth-order coefficient is positive sufficiently far out, both functions decay, and the cutoff error tends to zero. The $Q$-equation has source $-\lambda QW\phi f+QR=O(A/r^2)$. Its norm inequality retains the positive constant $c_D$ in addition to $k^2/2+u$, so $A/r^2$ is a supersolution at sufficiently large radius. This proves [eq:channel-leading-bounds](#eq:channel-leading-bounds).

Rotation fields $\Omega$ acting on $x$ commute with $\Delta_x,B,P,Q$. Although $\|\Omega^jW\|=O(r^{-1})$, one has $\|\Omega^jW\phi\|=O(r^{-2})$ for $j\geq1$, and $\Omega^ju=O_D(r^{-3})$. Suppose the two leading bounds hold for fewer than $s$ rotations. Every commutator source for $\Omega^sF$ is $O(A/r^2)$: its $P$-part uses the improved estimate on $W\phi$, and its $Q$-part uses $O(r^{-1})O(A/r^2)$. First the full norm comparison gives $\Omega^sF=O(A)$; then the $Q$-equation and its gap give $Q\Omega^sF=O(A/r^2)$. This closes the induction. The absolute decay needed for comparison follows from the cutoff construction, allowing a small exponential loss for the polynomial coefficients of rotation fields.

Set $h_0=f/A$, and define <a id="eq:angular-recursion-operators"></a>$$K_j=-\tfrac12\Delta_{\mathbb S^2}
       -\tfrac12(\beta-j)(\beta-j+1),\qquad
 c_m=U_m+\lambda\langle\phi,W_m\phi\rangle.
 \label{eq:angular-recursion-operators}$$ Axial symmetry and reflection give $c_2=0$. The projected equation conjugated by $A$ is <a id="eq:projected-radial-equation"></a>$$-\tfrac12h_0''+
 \left(k-\frac{\beta+1}{r}\right)h_0'
 +\frac{K_0+c_2}{r^2}h_0+\frac{c_3}{r^3}h_0+\cdots
 =O(r^{-4}),
 \label{eq:projected-radial-equation}$$ with any fixed number of angular derivatives. The off-diagonal source has this order because $\|PWQ\|=\|QW\phi\|=O(r^{-2})$ and $QF=O(A/r^2)$.

For the radial step, if a bounded angular-valued function $H$ satisfies $$-\tfrac12H''+\left(k-\frac{\beta+1}{r}\right)H'=G(r),
 \qquad G(r)=O(r^{-q}),\quad q>1,$$ then <a id="eq:radial-integrating-factor"></a>$$H'(r)=2e^{2kr}r^{-2(\beta+1)}
       \int_r^\infty e^{-2ks}s^{2(\beta+1)}G(s)\,ds.
 \label{eq:radial-integrating-factor}$$ The other homogeneous branch grows exponentially and is excluded by boundedness. The integral is $O(r^{-q})$, uniformly for the present bounded $\beta$ and positive lower bound on $k$; thus $H$ has a limit with error $O(r^{1-q})$. Initially the right side of the radial equation is $O(r^{-2})$, since all needed angular derivatives of $h_0$ are bounded. Hence $h_0\to a_0$, with error $O(r^{-1})$. Set <a id="eq:scalar-channel-recursion"></a>$$a_1=\frac{(K_0+c_2)a_0}{k},\qquad
 a_2=\frac{(K_1+c_2)a_1+c_3a_0}{2k}.
 \label{eq:scalar-channel-recursion}$$ Subtracting $a_0+a_1/r$ cancels order two; the integrating factor improves the remainder to $O(r^{-2})$, with derivative $O(r^{-3})$. Subtracting $a_2/r^2$ cancels order three, giving $\mathcal E=O(r^{-3})$ and $\mathcal E'=O(r^{-4})$. The equation gives $\mathcal E''=O(r^{-4})$. The same argument with finitely more angular derivatives at each step proves [eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives](#eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives). Reflection makes $f,a_j$, and all radial remainders odd. The mean-value theorem in the transverse angular coordinate gives the additional factor at the equator.

On a fixed radius, the projected function and its angular derivatives converge by the local estimates in [lem:channel-cutoff](#lem:channel-cutoff). Since $\|f/A-a_0\|_{C^1}\leq C_D/r$ uniformly, first choosing the radius large and then sending $\lambda$ to zero proves $C^1$ convergence of $a_0^\lambda$. At zero coupling the scalar equation for $e$ on the upper half-space has a subsolution which is a positive multiple of $$e^{-k_0r}r^{2/k_0-1}\omega_z(1+M/r).$$ After division by the radial factor and $\omega_z$, the controlling $r^{-2}$ coefficient contains $-k_0M$; it dominates the bounded angular and scalar corrections for large $M$. Positivity of $e$ on an inner hemisphere and the boundary point lemma on its equator supply a strictly positive starting multiple, including the equatorial limit. Exterior comparison proves $\inf a_0^0/\omega_z>0$. Oddness and $C^1$ convergence give [eq:simple-angular-zero](#eq:simple-angular-zero). The identical scalar argument for $b$, without an angular zero and with rate $\gamma$, proves [eq:fast-orbital-expansion](#eq:fast-orbital-expansion); positivity on the compact inner sphere gives $\min b_\infty>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### The first nonzero complementary term on the plane

The scalar channel’s oddness forces it to vanish on the median plane. We now identify the complementary term that supplies its missing density there, and control its error before comparing it with the retained fast term $bG$.

<a id="stmt-lem-plane-coefficient"></a>
<div id="lem:plane-coefficient" class="lemma">

**Lemma 9** (Complementary expansion and plane coefficient). *There are smooth exponentially localized $Q\mathcal K$-valued angular coefficients $T_2,T_3,T_4$, bounded uniformly for small $\lambda$, such that <a id="eq:complementary-expansion"></a>$$QF=bG+\lambda A
       \left(\frac{T_2}{r^2}+\frac{T_3}{r^3}+\frac{T_4}{r^4}\right)
       +\mathcal E_Q,\qquad
 \|\partial_x^\nu\mathcal E_Q\|
       \leq C_D\lambda Ar^{-5}\quad(|\nu|\leq2).
 \label{eq:complementary-expansion}$$ Writing $Y_z=y_{1z}+y_{2z}$, one has <a id="eq:equatorial-coefficient"></a>$$\begin{split}
 T_2&=-a_0B_Q^{-1}QW_2\phi,\\
 T_2|_{\omega_z=0}&=T_3|_{\omega_z=0}=0,\\
 T_4|_{\omega_z=0}
   &=\frac12B_Q^{-1}\Delta_{\mathbb S^2}T_2|_{\omega_z=0}
     =-a_0'(0)B_Q^{-2}QY_z\phi .
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:equatorial-coefficient}$$ The last vector has norm bounded below by a positive constant at fixed $D$, uniformly for sufficiently small $\lambda$. All $T_j$ have distinguished spin up.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-plane-coefficient"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-plane-coefficient</summary>

*Proof.* On localized angular coefficients define $$C_m=U_m\,\mathrm{id}_{Q\mathcal K}+\lambda QW_mQ.$$ The qualification is essential: $W_m$, and hence $C_m$, can be unbounded on arbitrary ionic vectors. Their action on the localized vectors in this proof is controlled by [eq:weighted-ionic-inverse](#eq:weighted-ionic-inverse), [eq:ionic-multipoles](#eq:ionic-multipoles). On a term $r^{-j}T(\omega)$, the conjugated $Q$-operator has expansion <a id="eq:complementary-recursion-action"></a>$$r^{-j}B_QT-jk r^{-j-1}T
 +r^{-j-2}K_jT+\sum_{m\geq2}r^{-j-m}C_mT.
 \label{eq:complementary-recursion-action}$$ The source from $P$, after division by $\lambda A$, is $-QW\phi\,f/A$. Its coefficients of orders two through four are $$\begin{split}
 S_2&=-a_0QW_2\phi,\\
 S_3&=-a_1QW_2\phi-a_0QW_3\phi,\\
 S_4&=-a_2QW_2\phi-a_1QW_3\phi-a_0QW_4\phi.
 \end{split}$$ We therefore define the coefficients by <a id="eq:complementary-recursion"></a>$$\begin{split}
 B_QT_2&=S_2,\\
 B_QT_3&=S_3+2kT_2,\\
 B_QT_4&=S_4+3kT_3-(K_2+C_2)T_2.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:complementary-recursion}$$ Repeated use of [eq:weighted-ionic-inverse](#eq:weighted-ionic-inverse), decreasing a positive weight after polynomial multiplication when necessary, gives smooth localized coefficients with every finite angular derivative bound used here. The nuclear quadrupole has already entered $a_2$ in [eq:scalar-channel-recursion](#eq:scalar-channel-recursion); the operator $C_3$ acts directly on this complementary expansion first at order five.

Every $S_j$ vanishes on the equator because its scalar factors $a_i$ are odd. The first two recursion equations give $T_2=T_3=0$ there. In the third equation all terms except the angular Laplacian of $T_2$ also vanish. At the equator $a_0=\Delta_{\mathbb S^2}a_0=0$, while $$\nabla_{\mathbb S^2}a_0=a_0'(0)e_z,\qquad
 \partial_{\omega_z}W_2=Y_z.$$ The product rule in $T_2=-a_0B_Q^{-1}QW_2\phi$ proves [eq:equatorial-coefficient](#eq:equatorial-coefficient), with its stated sign and factor. The slope is nonzero by [eq:simple-angular-zero](#eq:simple-angular-zero). Moreover $Y_z\phi$ is ionic-odd, so $QY_z\phi=Y_z\phi$. This vector cannot vanish: the zero set of the multiplier $Y_z$ is a measure-zero hyperplane, which cannot support a nonzero $L^2$ vector. Injectivity of $B_Q^{-2}$ proves nonvanishing. Continuity of the localized resolvents and vectors gives the uniform lower bound for small $\lambda$. All operators in the recursion preserve the distinguished spin, so each $T_j$ has spin up.

We next prove the remainder with its asserted uniformity. The retained vector $bG$ solves the homogeneous $Q$-equation at $\lambda=0$. Since $QG=G$, its perturbed residual is <a id="eq:fast-channel-residual"></a>$$\begin{split}
 \mathcal L_{\lambda,Q}(bG)
 &=bQ\left\{\lambda\left(|y_1-y_2|^{-1}+W\right)
                 -E_3(\lambda)+E_3(0)\right\}G\\
 &=O(\lambda b),
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:fast-channel-residual}$$ also through two ordinary $x$-derivatives. Hardy’s inequality controls the ionic Coulomb factor on $G$; the other $x$-dependent factors are smooth in the exterior. By [eq:channel-rate-separation](#eq:channel-rate-separation), [eq:fast-orbital-expansion](#eq:fast-orbital-expansion), <a id="eq:fast-term-absorption"></a>$$b/A\leq C_D e^{-(\gamma-k_0)r/2}r^{C_D}.
 \label{eq:fast-term-absorption}$$ Thus the residual is bounded by $C_D\lambda Ar^{-5}$, uniformly in $\lambda$. No equality of perturbed and unperturbed excited thresholds is being assumed.

The matched slow terms have residual $O(\lambda Ar^{-5})$. The same bound holds through two Cartesian derivatives: finite multipole remainders have symbol bounds on the localized coefficients, and the scalar remainder is multiplied by $\lambda QW\phi=O(\lambda r^{-2})$. The three bounds in [eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives](#eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives) suffice; one does not need the stronger assertion $\partial_r^2\mathcal E=O(r^{-5})$. The source $QR$ is exponentially smaller. Hence $\mathcal E_Q$ satisfies a $Q$-equation with a source of this size, including two derivatives. On a fixed large starting sphere its value is $O(\lambda)$, since $QF=bG$ at zero coupling and all fixed-radius data converge at that rate. The positive $Q$-gap makes a sufficiently large multiple of $\lambda Ar^{-5}$ a scalar norm supersolution which dominates both the source and boundary value. Exterior comparison gives the undifferentiated remainder bound.

For the derivative upgrade it is important to include the unbounded ionic operator. For compactly supported $v$ with values in $\operatorname{Dom}B_Q$, Fourier transform in $x$ and the spectral resolution of $B_Q\geq0$ give <a id="eq:positive-ionic-elliptic-estimate"></a>$$\|D_x^2v\|_2+\|B_Qv\|_2
 \leq C\|(-\Delta_x/2+B_Q)v\|_2.
 \label{eq:positive-ionic-elliptic-estimate}$$ Indeed $|\xi|^2$ and a spectral value $b\geq0$ are each bounded by a fixed multiple of $|\xi|^2/2+b$. For the local estimate, first control $\nabla_xv$ by the cutoff energy identity and then apply [eq:positive-ionic-elliptic-estimate](#eq:positive-ionic-elliptic-estimate) with a second cutoff. The remaining coefficient $k^2/2+u+\lambda QWQ$ and its $x$-derivatives are bounded operators on exterior unit balls, uniformly in their centers. Since $B_Q$ is independent of $x$, difference quotients and two further local estimates give $$\|\mathcal E_Q\|_{H_x^4(B(x,1/2);\mathcal K)}
 \leq C_D\lambda A(r)r^{-5}.$$ The source derivatives and the already proved pointwise bound control the right side on a larger ball; $A$ varies by a bounded factor there. Hilbert-valued Sobolev embedding in dimension three, $H^4%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21AA}%
  \KSOriginalHookrightarrow\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}C^{2,\alpha}$ for $\alpha<1/2$, gives the two pointwise derivative bounds in [eq:complementary-expansion](#eq:complementary-expansion). ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-13"></a>
#### Uniform comparison of the three density scales

The coefficient calculation concerns the eventual tail at a fixed positive coupling. To obtain a common exterior radius as the coupling tends to zero, we must also control the region where the fast term and the induced plane term are comparable. Keeping all three scales in the following lower bound makes that comparison uniform.

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-plane-coefficient-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-plane-coefficient</summary>

*Proof of [prop:density-properties](#prop:density-properties).* Put $t=\omega_z$ and $$T_{\mathrm{eq}}=T_4|_{t=0},\qquad
 M(x)=\phi f(x)+b(x)G+\lambda A(r)r^{-4}T_{\mathrm{eq}}.$$ Axial symmetry and [eq:equatorial-coefficient](#eq:equatorial-coefficient) make $T_{\mathrm{eq}}$ independent of equatorial azimuth. Smoothness and the zeros of $T_2,T_3$ give $$\|T_2(\omega)\|+\|T_3(\omega)\|
       +\|T_4(\omega)-T_{\mathrm{eq}}\|\leq C_D|t|.$$ The estimate holds on the whole sphere by compactness away from the equator. For any distinguished-spin-up $Q$-vector $S$, [eq:core-spin-components](#eq:core-spin-components) implies $$|\langle bG,S\rangle|
 \leq |b|\,\|S\|=2^{-1/2}\|bG\|\,\|S\|.$$ Therefore <a id="eq:spin-noncancellation"></a>$$\begin{split}
 (1-2^{-1/2})(2b^2+\|S\|^2)
 &\leq\|bG+S\|^2\\
 &\leq(1+2^{-1/2})(2b^2+\|S\|^2).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:spin-noncancellation}$$ The $P$-component is orthogonal to both these terms. The simple angular zero and positive lower bound on $\|T_{\mathrm{eq}}\|$ give $$\|M\|\geq c_D(A|t|+b+\lambda Ar^{-4}).$$ The complementary expansion gives $$\|F-M\|\leq C_D\lambda A(r^{-2}|t|+r^{-5})=o_D(\|M\|),$$ uniformly in small $\lambda$ as the radius increases. Consequently <a id="eq:three-scale-lower-bound"></a>$$\|F\|\geq c_D(A|\omega_z|+b+\lambda Ar^{-4}).
 \label{eq:three-scale-lower-bound}$$ In addition, there is the absolute bound <a id="eq:absolute-core-ratio"></a>$$b/\|F\|\leq2.
 \label{eq:absolute-core-ratio}$$ The distinguished-down-spin component of $M$ has norm $b$. Increase the radius so that $\|F-M\|\leq\|M\|/2$; then $$\|F\|\geq\|M\|-\|F-M\|\geq\|M\|/2\geq b/2.$$ Only the radius, not the constant $2$, depends on $D$. The cutoff errors are relatively negligible because $$e^{-2\sigma r}/b^2\longrightarrow0\qquad(\sigma>\gamma).$$ This proves exterior positivity of $n$; positivity on the remaining compact set follows from [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity) after decreasing $\lambda_D$. Symmetry, compact convergence, and exterior smoothness have already been proved.

We verify differentiated comparisons through the moving crossover. The projected remainders and their first two radial derivatives are odd, so their angular derivative bounds supply the factor $|t|$. Their errors can thus be divided by the first lower scale in [eq:three-scale-lower-bound](#eq:three-scale-lower-bound). For the complementary error, $$\frac{\|\partial_x^\nu\mathcal E_Q\|}{\|F\|}
 \leq C_D/r\quad(|\nu|\leq2,\ \lambda>0),$$ using the third lower scale. At $\lambda=0$ this error is zero. Differentiating the explicit slow terms with $\partial_r+k$ leaves only inverse powers of $r$. For the fast orbital, $$(\partial_r+k)b=(k-\gamma)b+O_D(b/r),\qquad
 (\partial_r+k)^2b=(k-\gamma)^2b+O_D(b/r).$$ It follows, with $\delta_\lambda=k-\gamma$, that <a id="eq:radial-relative-derivatives"></a>$$\begin{split}
 (\partial_r+k)F&=\delta_\lambda bG+o_D(\|F\|),\\
 (\partial_r+k)^2F&=\delta_\lambda^2bG+o_D(\|F\|),
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:radial-relative-derivatives}$$ where the ratios denoted by $o_D(1)$ tend to zero uniformly in the chosen coupling interval.

At fixed $z$, $\partial_p$ has radial part $(p/r)\partial_r$ and angular part of size $O(|t|/r)$ on $0\leq z\leq p/2$. Applying it once or twice to smooth odd angular coefficients retains a factor $|t|$, together with the corresponding inverse powers of $r$. Thus $$\left(\partial_p+k\frac p r\right)F
 =\delta_\lambda\frac p r\,bG+o_D(\|F\|).$$ On $0\leq z\leq L$, $1-p/r=O_L(p^{-2})$ is negligible. With $D_p=\partial_p+k$, <a id="eq:horizontal-relative-derivatives"></a>$$D_pF=\delta_\lambda bG+o_D(\|F\|),\qquad
 D_p^2F=\delta_\lambda^2bG+o_D(\|F\|).
 \label{eq:horizontal-relative-derivatives}$$ These estimates include angular derivatives of the displayed $T_j$: their zeros are retained for $T_2,T_3$, whereas the extra angular factor for $T_4$ is bounded using the first lower scale. The ordinary derivative bounds handle $\mathcal E_Q$ directly.

By [eq:absolute-core-ratio](#eq:absolute-core-ratio), $\|bG\|/\|F\|\leq2\sqrt2$. The identity $$(\partial_r+2k)\|F\|^2
 =2\operatorname{Re}\langle F,(\partial_r+k)F\rangle$$ and its horizontal version bound the normalized first derivatives by $4\sqrt2|\delta_\lambda|+o_D(1)$. Also $$(\partial_p+2k)^2\|F\|^2
 =2\|D_pF\|^2+
  2\operatorname{Re}\langle F,D_p^2F\rangle$$ gives the normalized second-derivative bound $(16+4\sqrt2)\delta_\lambda^2+o_D(1)$ in the strip. The differentiated cutoff errors remain relatively negligible. In particular, there is $\rho_D(R)\downarrow0$, independent of small $\lambda$, such that <a id="eq:quantitative-density-drift"></a>$$\begin{split}
 \max\Bigg\{&
 \sup_{r\geq R}|\partial_r\log n+2k|,\ 
 \sup_{\substack{r\geq R\\0\leq z\leq p/2}}
       \left|\partial_p\log n+2k\frac p r\right|,\\
 &\sup_{\substack{p\geq R\\0\leq z\leq L}}
       \frac{|m_p|+|m_{pp}|}{m}\Bigg\}
 \leq100(|\gamma-k|+|\gamma-k|^2)+\rho_D(R).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:quantitative-density-drift}$$ All $D$-dependent coefficients other than the fast exponential rate have entered the vanishing function $\rho_D$. Since $\gamma-k_0\to0$ as $D\to\infty$ and $k\to k_0$ as $\lambda\downarrow0$, this proves the derivative assertions in [eq:density-drift](#eq:density-drift), [eq:strip-data](#eq:strip-data).

For the vertical ratio the expansions give, uniformly on $0\leq z\leq L$, <a id="eq:strip-comparison-vector"></a>$$\begin{split}
 F(p,z)={}&\phi a_0'(0)A(p)z/p+b(p,0)G
               +\lambda A(p)p^{-4}T_{\mathrm{eq}}\\
 &+o_D\bigl(A(p)z/p+b(p,0)+\lambda A(p)p^{-4}\bigr).
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:strip-comparison-vector}$$ The last coefficient tends to zero uniformly in $\lambda,z$, and the error is relatively small compared with the norm of the three-term comparison vector by [eq:spin-noncancellation](#eq:spin-noncancellation). Indeed $r-p=O_L(p^{-1})$; after including the lower radial terms, the projected factor is $a_0'(0)z/p+O_D(z/p^2)$; and $b(p,z)/b(p,0)=1+O_D(p^{-1})$. The $T_2,T_3$ terms are $O_D(\lambda A z/p^3)$; all other errors have the displayed bound by [eq:three-scale-lower-bound](#eq:three-scale-lower-bound). The squared comparison norm lies between $1-1/\sqrt2$ and $1+1/\sqrt2$ times $$|a_0'(0)|^2A(p)^2z^2/p^2+
 2b(p,0)^2+\lambda^2A(p)^2p^{-8}\|T_{\mathrm{eq}}\|^2.$$ This expression is nondecreasing in $z$. The ratio of the constants is $3+2\sqrt2<6$; after absorbing uniformly small errors, the ratio in [eq:strip-data](#eq:strip-data) is at most $20$.

Now fix $\lambda>0$. On the plane $f=0$ and $T_2=T_3=0$. Since $b=o(Ap^{-4})$, the complementary expansion yields $$F(p,0)=\lambda A(p)p^{-4}T_{\mathrm{eq}}+o(A(p)p^{-4}).$$ The coefficient is nonzero and the density cutoff error is exponentially smaller, proving [eq:plane-tail](#eq:plane-tail).

For plane-centered $x_j=p_j\omega_j$, put $r_j(x)=|x_j+x|$. Uniformly on compact displacement sets, $$r_j(x)-p_j\to\omega_*\cdot x,\qquad
 p_jz/r_j(x)\to z,\qquad
 A(r_j(x))/A(p_j)\to e^{-k\omega_*\cdot x}.$$ The projected expansion and its simple angular zero imply $$\frac{PF(x_j+x)}{A(p_j)/p_j}
 \longrightarrow\phi a_0'(0)z e^{-k\omega_*\cdot x}.$$ The complementary part divided by this normalization tends uniformly to zero. Its fast term is exponentially smaller; the $T_2,T_3$ terms retain the angular zero and the plane term is $O(Ap_j^{-4})$. The cutoff error is negligible. Squaring gives [eq:plane-translate](#eq:plane-translate) with $a=|a_0'(0)|>0$.

Finally suppose $r_j=|x_j|\to\infty$ and $|z_j|\to\infty$. Reflection reduces the proof to $z_j>0$. If $\omega_{j,z}$ stays bounded below, the conclusion follows directly from the smooth nonzero projected angular coefficient. Otherwise $t_j=z_j/r_j\to0$, and $$\frac{b(r_j)}{A(r_j)t_j}\to0,\qquad
 \frac{\lambda A(r_j)r_j^{-4}}{A(r_j)t_j}
       =\frac{\lambda}{r_j^3z_j}\to0.$$ The $T_2,T_3$ terms are relatively negligible as well. For bounded $x$, $t_j(x)/t_j=1+O(1/z_j+1/r_j)$. Since $a_0(t)/t$ is continuous and bounded away from zero through the equator, the angular amplitude has relative variation tending to one. The radial ratio tends to $e^{-k\omega_*\cdot x}$; all lower terms are uniformly negligible. Squaring proves [eq:bulk-translate](#eq:bulk-translate). ◻

</details>

We record one more zero-coupling consequence for the next section: <a id="eq:unperturbed-strip-ratio"></a>$$\sup_{0<z\leq10}
 \left\{
 \left|\partial_p\log\frac be+(\gamma-k_0)\frac p r\right|
 +\left|\partial_p^2\log\frac be\right|
 \right\}\longrightarrow0\qquad(p\to\infty).
 \label{eq:unperturbed-strip-ratio}$$ Indeed the scalar expansions have the strip form $$e(p,z)=z e^{-k_0r}r^{2/k_0-2}c(r,z/r),\qquad
 b(p,z)=e^{-\gamma r}r^{2/\gamma-1}d(r,z/r),$$ where $c,d$ are smooth, bounded above and below by positive constants, and have the already proved radial and angular derivative estimates. The term $-\log z$ in $\log(b/e)$ disappears under $\partial_p$. The other terms give $\partial_p\log(b/e)=-(\gamma-k_0)p/r+O_D(r^{-1})$ and $\partial_p^2\log(b/e)=O_D(r^{-2})+O_D(z^2/r^3)$. This proves the assertion uniformly down to the simple zero of $e$.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### A fixed-parameter version

The exact charge will be defined by forward spectral and variational tests. Passing them need not put its reciprocal in a particular perturbative interval chosen earlier. The next proposition isolates the hypotheses needed for the final tail argument.

<a id="stmt-prop-fixed-parameter-channels"></a>
<div id="prop:fixed-parameter-channels" class="proposition">

**Proposition 10** (Channels at a fixed coupling). *Suppose $D$ satisfies [prop:two-centre-spectrum](#prop:two-centre-spectrum) and $$k_0>\sqrt{0.98},\qquad \gamma<\sqrt{49/45}.$$ Fix $t\in(0,1]$. Assume the two-electron ground level is isolated and simple with an even ionic singlet, and the three-electron ground level in $S_z=1/2$ is isolated and simple, is an absolute ground level, and has a real axisymmetric odd ground state. Assume $n_t>0$, and set $$k=\sqrt{2(E_2(t)-E_3(t))},\qquad
 A_t(r)=e^{-kr}r^{(2-2t)/k-1}.$$ Suppose <a id="eq:fixed-channel-hypotheses"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 2\epsilon_b-E_3(t)>0.49,\qquad 0.99<k<\gamma,\\
 \inf_{\omega_z>0}\liminf_{r\to\infty}
 \frac{\|P_tF_{\mathrm{true},t}(r\omega)\|}
      {\omega_z A_t(r)}>0.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:fixed-channel-hypotheses}$$ Then $n_t$ has the local regularity in [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity), is smooth away from the nuclei, and satisfies [eq:plane-tail](#eq:plane-tail), [eq:plane-translate](#eq:plane-translate), [eq:bulk-translate](#eq:bulk-translate) at this fixed coupling, with $A=A_t$ and suitable positive constants. Membership of $t$ in the preceding perturbative interval is not required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-fixed-parameter-channels"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-fixed-parameter-channels</summary>

*Proof.* Local regularity uses only the Coulomb eigenvalue equation, and exterior smoothness follows from the cluster argument in [lem:channel-cutoff](#lem:channel-cutoff). The isolated ionic ground supplies a positive $Q$-gap and [eq:weighted-ionic-inverse](#eq:weighted-ionic-inverse), with constants now allowed to depend on $t$.

The configuration-space lower bound [eq:configuration-exterior-bound](#eq:configuration-exterior-bound) remains valid because $t\geq0$. The energy hypothesis permits weights with exponent arbitrarily close to $\sqrt{0.98}$. Since $$\sqrt{0.98}\sqrt{10/9}=\sqrt{49/45}>\gamma$$ and $k_0>\sqrt{0.98}$, the true and unperturbed states admit the same cutoff construction with residual exponent $\sigma>\gamma$. There is no need to estimate the difference uniformly as $t\downarrow0$: at this fixed positive $t$, its finite weighted norms and the fixed energy difference divided by $t$ are part of the constants. Thus [eq:cutoff-channel-equation](#eq:cutoff-channel-equation), [eq:channel-residual](#eq:channel-residual), [eq:density-cutoff-error](#eq:density-cutoff-error) hold at the chosen parameter.

The norm comparisons, angular induction, and integrating-factor argument use only the positive ionic gap, bounded exterior coefficients, and exponential residual. They give [eq:projected-channel-expansion](#eq:projected-channel-expansion), [eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives](#eq:scalar-remainder-derivatives) without a perturbative assertion about $a_0$. Also $\|F_{\mathrm{true}}-F\|=O(e^{-\sigma r})$, by the same weighted local estimates on the tail support. For each fixed $\omega_z>0$, the liminf in [eq:fixed-channel-hypotheses](#eq:fixed-channel-hypotheses) is therefore $|a_0(\omega)|/\omega_z$. Its positive infimum and the smooth odd extension imply $$\inf_{\omega_z>0}\frac{|a_0(\omega)|}{\omega_z}>0,\qquad
 |a_0'(0)|>0.$$ The sign of $a_0$ is constant on the upper hemisphere and does not affect the density.

Repeat the complementary recursion [eq:complementary-recursion](#eq:complementary-recursion). The strict inequality $k<\gamma$ absorbs the residual of $bG$ into $tAr^{-5}$; the stronger uniform separation in [eq:channel-rate-separation](#eq:channel-rate-separation) is unnecessary. On a fixed starting sphere the remainder is finite; dividing its bound by the positive profile $tA_t(r)r^{-5}$ is absorbed in the allowed $t$-dependent comparison constant. The norm and differentiated comparisons give [eq:complementary-expansion](#eq:complementary-expansion) with $t$-dependent constants. Finally $QY_z\phi=Y_z\phi%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=2260}%
  \KSOriginalNe\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}0$, so the coefficient in [eq:equatorial-coefficient](#eq:equatorial-coefficient) is nonzero without continuity from zero coupling. The fixed-coupling parts of the preceding density proof give the plane lower bound and both translation limits. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:angle"></a>
### An angle minimum and its spectral gap

We now construct two spatial orbitals with the prescribed density. The main point is to prove a spectral gap for their common formal potential. This will allow the next section to compare the resulting determinant with arbitrary fermionic ensembles.

Write $\Omega_+=\{x\in\mathbf R^3:z>0\}$. For a positive continuous weight $w$, set $$H^1(w)=
 \left\{\xi\in H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R^3):
       \int_{\mathbf R^3}w\bigl(|\xi|^2+|\nabla\xi|^2\bigr)<\infty\right\},
 \qquad
 \mathcal E_w(\xi)=\frac12\int_{\mathbf R^3}w|\nabla\xi|^2.$$ When $w$ is integrable, use the unnormalized weighted variance $$\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_w(\xi)=
 \int_{\mathbf R^3}w|\xi|^2
 -\frac{\left|\int_{\mathbf R^3}w\xi\right|^2}{\int_{\mathbf R^3}w}.$$ The corresponding spaces on $\Omega_+$ are defined in the same way, with local Sobolev regularity up to its boundary when a trace is specified. On compact sets all these weighted norms are equivalent to the ordinary Sobolev norms. Radial cutoffs, followed by local smooth approximation, are dense in the full-space form norm. Indeed, for cutoffs $\chi_R$ with $|\nabla\chi_R|\le C/R$, the additional gradient term is bounded by $C R^{-2}\int_{|x|>R}w|\xi|^2$. For odd functions the analogous approximation on $\Omega_+$ uses their zero trace on the median plane.

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### The constrained angle

<a id="stmt-prop-angle-minimizer"></a>
<div id="prop:angle-minimizer" class="proposition">

**Proposition 11** (Existence and regularity of the angle). *Let $n$ be a positive, continuous, even density with $\int n=3$, $n\in H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R^3)$, and $\sqrt n\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)$. The problem <a id="eq:angle-minimum"></a>$$\label{eq:angle-minimum}
 \begin{aligned}
 &\min_{\vartheta\in H^1(n;\mathbf R)}
       \int_{\mathbf R^3}n|\nabla\vartheta|^2,\\
 &\text{subject to }\quad
       \vartheta\text{ odd},\qquad
       \int_{\mathbf R^3}n\sin^2\vartheta=1
 \end{aligned}$$ has a minimizer $\theta$ satisfying $0\le\theta\le\pi/2$ on $\Omega_+$. Every such folded minimizer has a multiplier $d>0$ and satisfies <a id="eq:angle-euler"></a>$$\label{eq:angle-euler}
 L_n\theta:=n^{-1}\operatorname{div}(n\nabla\theta)
       =-d\sin(2\theta)
 \quad\hbox{on }\mathbf R^3.$$ It belongs locally to $W^{2,p}$ for every $p<6$, and $$0<\theta<\pi/2\quad\hbox{on }\Omega_+.$$ In particular, it is locally $C^{1,\alpha}$ for every $0<\alpha<1/2$. Axial symmetry of $n$ is not required for this proposition, and axial symmetry of $\theta$ is not asserted.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-angle-minimizer"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-angle-minimizer</summary>

*Proof.* On the upper half-space the Lipschitz map $t%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \KSOriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}\arcsin|\sin t|$ preserves $\sin^2t$, preserves zero trace, and does not increase gradient energy. Extend the folded function oddly. Thus every competitor can be replaced by one taking values in $[0,\pi/2]$ above the plane.

The admissible class is nonempty. Smooth odd angles that equal $\pi/2$ on increasingly large compact subsets of $\Omega_+$ have constraint values approaching $3$. For one such angle the constraint is greater than $1$; multiplying it by a parameter in $[0,1]$ and using continuity produces the required value $1$.
