A folded minimizing sequence is bounded in $H^1$ on every compact set, since $n$ has a positive local lower bound. Local compactness gives an odd limit with zero trace on the plane. The bound $\sin^2\theta\le1$ and integrability of $n$ preserve the constraint under exhaustion of space. Lower semicontinuity proves minimality. The bound on the angle also gives its membership in $L^2(n)$.

Folding any perturbed competitor shows that a folded minimum is a minimum for unrestricted odd variations. The constraint derivative is nonzero on some compact smooth odd variation. Otherwise $\sin(2\theta)=0$ almost everywhere on $\Omega_+$. A locally Sobolev function with values in the discrete set $\{0,\pi/2\}$ has zero gradient and is constant on that connected set; the trace and constraint exclude both possibilities. The Lagrange-multiplier rule, normalized by a factor of two, therefore gives $$\int n\nabla\theta\cdot\nabla\varphi
       =d\int n\sin(2\theta)\varphi$$ for compact odd tests. The same identity for even tests is zero on both sides by parity, proving the full-space equation.

Testing with cutoffs times the bounded function $\theta$ gives <a id="eq:angle-positive-multiplier"></a>$$\label{eq:angle-positive-multiplier}
 \int n|\nabla\theta|^2
       =d\int n\theta\sin(2\theta).$$ The cutoff error tends to zero by finite gradient energy and $\int n<\infty$. The left side is positive. The integral on the right without $d$ is positive by folding, oddness, and the preceding nonconstancy argument. Hence $d>0$.

Here are the local estimates needed below. Positivity of $n$ and $n\in H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$ imply $\nabla\log n\in L^6_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Rewrite [eq:angle-euler](#eq:angle-euler) by testing with $\varphi/n$, where $\varphi$ is compactly supported and smooth. The positive local lower bound and $H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$ regularity of $n$ make this a legitimate local $H^1$ test. Thus, in distributions, $$\Delta\theta
   =-(\nabla\log n)\cdot\nabla\theta-d\sin(2\theta).$$ Its right side initially lies in $L^{3/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}$. Interior estimates for the ordinary Laplacian on nested compact sets <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Yuan19](#ref-Yuan19), pp. 6–7] give $$W^{2,3/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}
 \ \Longrightarrow\ \nabla\theta\in L^3_{\mathrm{loc}}
 \ \Longrightarrow\ H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}
 \ \Longrightarrow\ W^{2,3}_{\mathrm{loc}}.$$ The last space gives $\nabla\theta\in L^q_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every finite $q$, and a final application of the equation gives $W^{2,p}_{\mathrm{loc}}$ for every $p<6$. These estimates are uniform when the local $H^2$ bounds and positive lower bounds for $n$, the local angle energy, and $d$ are uniform. They apply across the median plane because the equation has already been extended to full space.

Finally, $\theta\ge0$ solves an equation with bounded coefficient $d\sin(2\theta)/\theta$, and $\delta=\pi/2-\theta\ge0$ on $\Omega_+$ satisfies <a id="eq:delta-equation"></a>$$\label{eq:delta-equation}
 L_n\delta=q\delta,\qquad
 q=d\,\frac{\sin(2\delta)}{\delta}\in[0,2d],$$ with the continuous value $2d$ at $\delta=0$. On each compact ball the divergence-form coefficient $nI$ is bounded and uniformly elliptic, and the zeroth-order coefficients are bounded. The local Harnack inequality therefore excludes an interior zero of either nonnegative function <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[SimonPDE](#ref-SimonPDE), Lecture 17, p. 199, and Lecture 18, Theorem 1]: the resulting constant would violate the trace or the constraint. ◻

</details>

The hypotheses of [prop:angle-minimizer](#prop:angle-minimizer) hold for the densities under consideration by [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity), [prop:density-properties](#prop:density-properties). In the remainder of the qualitative argument, $D$ is fixed and sufficiently large and $\lambda$ tends to zero.

<a id="stmt-prop-angle-convergence"></a>
<div id="prop:angle-convergence" class="proposition">

**Proposition 12** (Convergence of every folded minimum). *For every choice of folded minimizing angles for $n_\lambda$, <a id="eq:angle-convergence"></a>$$\label{eq:angle-convergence}
 \theta_\lambda\longrightarrow
 \theta_*=\arctan\!\left(\frac{e}{\sqrt2\,b}\right),
 \qquad
 d_\lambda\longrightarrow d_*=\epsilon_e-\epsilon_b.$$ The angle convergence is weak in local $H^1$, strong in local $L^2$, and in $C^1$ on every fixed compact set. In particular, all these conclusions hold uniformly over the set of folded minimizers in the sequential sense.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-angle-convergence"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-angle-convergence</summary>

*Proof.* At $\lambda=0$, let $g=\sqrt{n_0}\cos\vartheta$ and $h=\sqrt{n_0}\sin\vartheta$ for an admissible folded angle. Their squared norms are $2$ and $1$; they are orthogonal by parity. The determinant with occupied spin orbitals $(g/\sqrt2)\uparrow,(g/\sqrt2)\downarrow,h\uparrow$ has density $n_0$ and kinetic energy $$\frac12\int\bigl(|\nabla g|^2+|\nabla h|^2\bigr)
 =\frac12\int|\nabla\sqrt{n_0}|^2
       +\frac12\int n_0|\nabla\vartheta|^2.$$ Adding the fixed integral $\int u n_0$ and using the simple three-electron ground state in the indicated spin sector shows that the minimum is attained uniquely by $g=\sqrt2 b,\ h=e$, with the signs fixed by folding. Indeed, equality fixes the occupied spin-orbital subspace; its one-dimensional down-spin part fixes $g$, and the remaining up-spin orbital then fixes $h$. This gives precisely $\theta_*$.

To obtain a recovery sequence for small $\lambda$, first multiply the bounded $\theta_*$ by radial cutoffs. Its finite angle energy and the tail of $n_0$ make the resulting energy and constraint errors tend to zero. Approximate on the remaining compact set by smooth odd functions. A fixed compact odd variation with nonzero constraint derivative corrects the constraint exactly, both at zero and at nearby $\lambda$. Local convergence of $n_\lambda$ then gives the energy upper bound for minima. Folding the corrected competitor, if necessary, only decreases its energy.

The minimizing angles consequently have uniformly bounded local $H^1$ norms. Common tightness of $n_\lambda$, supplied by [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity), preserves the constraint for any local limit. Lower semicontinuity and the recovery bound make that limit a minimum at zero coupling, hence $\theta_*$. For a fixed compact odd $\varphi$ with nonzero limiting constraint derivative, the weak equation gives $$d_\lambda
 =\frac{\int n_\lambda\nabla\theta_\lambda\cdot\nabla\varphi}
        {\int n_\lambda\sin(2\theta_\lambda)\varphi}.$$ Its numerator and denominator converge. The limiting orbital equations for $b,e$ identify the quotient as $d_*$. The uniform local estimates in [prop:angle-minimizer](#prop:angle-minimizer), together with compact Sobolev embedding, now give $C^1$ convergence on compact sets. Applying this argument to every sequence of minimizers proves the asserted uniformity over all folded choices. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-orbital-equations"></a>
<div id="prop:orbital-equations" class="proposition">

**Proposition 13** (The two orbital equations). *For a folded minimizer define <a id="eq:angle-orbitals"></a>$$\label{eq:angle-orbitals}
 g=\sqrt n\cos\theta,\qquad h=\sqrt n\sin\theta.$$ Then $g,h\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)\cap H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R^3)$, $g$ is even and strictly positive, and $h$ is odd and strictly positive on $\Omega_+$. Moreover, $$\|g\|_2^2=2,\qquad \|h\|_2^2=1,\qquad
 \langle g,h\rangle=0,\qquad g^2+h^2=n.$$ The real function <a id="eq:formal-potential"></a>$$\label{eq:formal-potential}
 V=\frac{\Delta g}{2g}
   =\frac{\Delta\sqrt n}{2\sqrt n}
        -\frac{|\nabla\theta|^2}{2}+d\sin^2\theta$$ belongs to $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R^3)$, and <a id="eq:orbital-equations"></a>$$\label{eq:orbital-equations}
 (-\Delta/2+V)g=0,\qquad
 (-\Delta/2+V)h=dh$$ in distributions. On the median plane, $g=\sqrt n$ and $h=0$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-orbital-equations"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-orbital-equations</summary>

*Proof.* The norm, parity, and positivity statements follow from the constraint and [prop:angle-minimizer](#prop:angle-minimizer). The Sobolev chain rule gives <a id="eq:angle-kinetic-identity"></a>$$\label{eq:angle-kinetic-identity}
 |\nabla g|^2+|\nabla h|^2
       =|\nabla\sqrt n|^2+n|\nabla\theta|^2,$$ so both orbitals belong to $H^1$. Positivity and local $H^2$ regularity of $n$, together with the angle estimates, give local $H^2$ regularity of the orbitals. Differentiating [eq:angle-orbitals](#eq:angle-orbitals) and using [eq:angle-euler](#eq:angle-euler) proves [eq:formal-potential](#eq:formal-potential), [eq:orbital-equations](#eq:orbital-equations). Since the continuous function $g$ has a positive lower bound on each compact set, $\Delta g/(2g)$ is locally square integrable. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-17"></a>
#### A strip comparison and exterior coercivity

The orbital equations alone do not establish their spectral ordering. We first control tests supported far from the nuclei, including tests with nonzero trace on the median plane. This will prevent weighted norm from escaping when we compare the even spectrum with its zero-coupling limit.

Fix throughout this subsection $$L=10,\qquad \mu=\frac1{10}.$$ The density is axisymmetric, so it may be written as $n(p,z)$. This notation will not be imposed on the angle.

<a id="stmt-lem-strip-solution"></a>
<div id="lem:strip-solution" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (The one-dimensional comparison function). *Suppose, for $p\ge P_0$ and $0\le z\le L$, that $m(p,z)=e^{2kp}n(p,z)>0$ satisfies <a id="eq:strip-comparison-data"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-comparison-data}
 \begin{aligned}
 |m_p|+|m_{pp}|&\le\eta m,\qquad 0<\eta\le1,\\
 \frac{m(p,t)}{m(p,s)}&\le C
       \qquad(0\le t\le s\le L).
 \end{aligned}$$ Let $U(p,\cdot)$ solve <a id="eq:strip-ode"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-ode}
 n^{-1}(nU_z)_z=\mu^2U,\qquad U(p,0)=1,\quad U(p,L)=0.$$ It is positive and decreasing on $0\le z<L$, and <a id="eq:strip-horizontal-derivatives"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-horizontal-derivatives}
 |U_p|+|U_{pp}|\le C_2\eta U,$$ where $C_2$ depends only on $C,L,\mu$. When $C\le100$, one may take $C_2<10^{30}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-strip-solution"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-strip-solution</summary>

*Proof.* Solve backward with final flux equal to one. If $J$ is the positive flux magnitude, the unnormalized solution and its flux obey <a id="eq:strip-volterra"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-volterra}
 \begin{aligned}
 \widetilde U(p,z)&=\int_z^L\frac{J(p,s)}{m(p,s)}\,ds,\\
 J(p,z)&=1+\mu^2\int_z^L\int_t^L
                \frac{m(p,t)}{m(p,s)}J(p,s)\,ds\,dt.
 \end{aligned}$$ The successive-integral series is positive. Its term of order $l$ is bounded by $$\frac{(\mu^2 C)^l(L-z)^{2l}}{(2l)!}.$$ It therefore converges uniformly, defines $J\ge1$, and gives the unique solution after normalization $U(p,z)=\widetilde U(p,z)/\widetilde U(p,0)$. The formulas give $U>0$ and $U_z<0$.

For a kernel $K=m(p,t)/m(p,s)$, $$|K_p|\le2\eta K,\qquad |K_{pp}|\le6\eta K.$$ The product of $l$ such kernels has derivatives of either positive order at most two bounded by $20(1+l)^2\eta$ times that product. Termwise differentiation of the series is consequently justified. For $C\le100$, the undifferentiated terms are bounded by $10^{2l}/(2l)!$, and $$\sum_{l\ge0}20(1+l)^2\frac{10^{2l}}{(2l)!}<10^8.$$ Since $J\ge1$, these estimates bound its first two $p$-derivatives relative to $J$. Also $|(m^{-1})_p|\le\eta m^{-1}$ and $|(m^{-1})_{pp}|\le3\eta m^{-1}$. Integrating the positive expression for $\widetilde U$, and then differentiating its quotient by $\widetilde U(p,0)$, proves [eq:strip-horizontal-derivatives](#eq:strip-horizontal-derivatives); the displayed numerical bounds give $C_2<10^{30}$ with ample slack. For general fixed $C$ the same convergent series gives a finite constant. Only the one-sided ratios in [eq:strip-comparison-data](#eq:strip-comparison-data) were used. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-exterior-gap"></a>
<div id="prop:exterior-gap" class="proposition">

**Proposition 15** (Uniform exterior gap). *For sufficiently large $D$, there are $\lambda_0>0$ and $R_0<\infty$ such that every folded minimizing angle for $0\le\lambda<\lambda_0$ satisfies <a id="eq:exterior-weighted-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:exterior-weighted-gap}
 \frac12\int_{\Omega_+}g^2|\nabla\xi|^2
       \ge0.03\int_{\Omega_+}g^2|\xi|^2$$ for tests supported in $\{|x|>R_0\}$. No zero trace on $z=0$ is required. The inequality holds throughout the corresponding weighted form domain.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-exterior-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-exterior-gap</summary>

*Proof.* Set $\delta=\pi/2-\theta$ on $\Omega_+$. By [eq:delta-equation](#eq:delta-equation), $$L_n\delta=q\delta,\quad 0\le q\le2d,\qquad
 \frac4{\pi^2}n\delta^2\le g^2=n\sin^2\delta\le n\delta^2.$$ Choose $D$ large enough that $d_*$ and $\gamma-k_0$ are small. The multiplier convergence and the density estimates [eq:density-drift](#eq:density-drift), [eq:strip-data](#eq:strip-data) allow us to choose the small constants below uniformly for sufficiently small $\lambda$.

First, $U$ from [lem:strip-solution](#lem:strip-solution) is a subsolution in the strip. In cylindrical coordinates, $$L_nU=\mu^2U+U_{pp}
          +(p^{-1}+\partial_p\log n)U_p.$$ The horizontal coefficient is bounded sufficiently far out by [eq:density-drift](#eq:density-drift). Thus [eq:strip-horizontal-derivatives](#eq:strip-horizontal-derivatives), with $\eta$ small, and $q\le2d$, with $d$ small, imply <a id="eq:strip-subsolution"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-subsolution}
 (L_n-q)U\ge0.$$

We next supply an inner boundary for comparison. At zero coupling put $\delta_*=\pi/2-\theta_*$. On $z>0$, $$\delta_*=\arctan(\sqrt2 b/e).$$ The scalar exterior estimates in [eq:unperturbed-strip-ratio](#eq:unperturbed-strip-ratio) show that, uniformly for $0<z\le L$, the first two horizontal logarithmic derivatives of $b/e$ are small when $D$ and then the starting radius are large. This remains true down to $z=0$: the simple odd factor of $e$ is canceled before taking a horizontal derivative. If $t>0$, the ratios of $t(\arctan t)'$ and $t^2(\arctan t)''$ to $\arctan t$ are bounded. Consequently $$\frac{|(\delta_*)_p|+|(\delta_*)_{pp}|}{\delta_*}$$ is uniformly small in the strip. Combining this with its angle equation gives, for a fixed sufficiently large $P_1$, <a id="eq:inner-strip-inequality"></a>$$\label{eq:inner-strip-inequality}
 n_0^{-1}\partial_z(n_0\partial_z\delta_*)
       <\mu^2\delta_*
       \quad(p=P_1,\ 0\le z\le L).$$ The strict inequality follows by making the horizontal error and $2d_*$ smaller than $\mu^2$. One-dimensional comparison with $U_0(P_1,\cdot)$ now gives $\delta_*/(\pi/2)>U_0$ for $0<z\le L$, and the derivative of their difference at $z=0$ is strictly positive. Its quotient by $z$ therefore has a positive minimum on $0<z\le L$. By [prop:angle-convergence](#prop:angle-convergence) and continuity of the one-dimensional problem in its positive coefficient, this boundary ordering persists for small $\lambda$, uniformly over all folded minimizers and all azimuths. For this use of continuity, [eq:strip-volterra](#eq:strip-volterra) gives uniform convergence of $U_\lambda$ and $(U_\lambda)_z$ on the fixed segment from uniform convergence and a positive lower bound for its density.

It follows that <a id="eq:strip-angle-comparison"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-angle-comparison}
 \frac{\delta(x)}{\pi/2}\ge U(p,z)
       \quad(p\ge P_1,\ 0\le z\le L).$$ Here is a comparison proof that also addresses infinity. The difference $v=\delta/(\pi/2)-U$ satisfies $(L_n-q)v\le0$ and has nonnegative finite-boundary values. Test this inequality with a horizontal cutoff squared times its negative part, with the appropriate negative sign. The resulting Caccioppoli inequality is $$\int n\chi_R^2|\nabla v_-|^2
       +\int nq\chi_R^2v_-^2
       \le4\int n v_-^2|\nabla\chi_R|^2.$$ The right side tends to zero because $v_-$ is bounded and $n$ is integrable. Thus $v_-$ is constant and its zero boundary trace makes it zero. No value at infinity has been imposed. This proof also makes clear that $\theta$ need not be axisymmetric: the coefficient $q$ may depend on azimuth. Differentiating the boundary inequality at $z=0$ gives <a id="eq:strip-flux-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-flux-bound}
 -\frac{\delta_z}{\delta}\le-U_z(p,0).$$

For a real smooth test $\xi$ compactly supported sufficiently far out, put $F_1=\delta\xi$. Integration by parts, with outward normal $-e_z$ on the plane, yields <a id="eq:delta-transform-boundary"></a>$$\label{eq:delta-transform-boundary}
 \int_{\Omega_+}n\delta^2|\nabla\xi|^2
 =\int_{\Omega_+}n\bigl(|\nabla F_1|^2+q|F_1|^2\bigr)
  -\int_{z=0}n\left(-\frac{\delta_z}{\delta}\right)|F_1|^2.$$ The energy of the solution of [eq:strip-ode](#eq:strip-ode) is $$\int_0^L n(U_z^2+\mu^2U^2)\,dz=-n(p,0)U_z(p,0).$$ It minimizes this energy among functions with the same endpoint values. Use the trial function $\cos(az)F_1$, $a=\pi/(2L)$, for each fixed horizontal point. The exact identity $$|F_1'|^2+a^2|F_1|^2
       -|(\cos(az)F_1)'|^2
       =|\sin(az)F_1'+a\cos(az)F_1|^2$$ gives <a id="eq:strip-boundary-cost"></a>$$\label{eq:strip-boundary-cost}
 n(p,0)(-U_z(p,0))|F_1(0)|^2
 \le\int_0^L n\bigl(
        |\partial_zF_1|^2+(\mu^2+a^2)|F_1|^2\bigr)\,dz.$$ Thus the boundary term spends at most the vertical gradient energy in the strip, together with the indicated $L^2$ cost.

The remaining gradient energy controls $\int_{\Omega_+}n|Y\cdot\nabla F_1|^2$, where $Y$ is the horizontal radial unit vector for $z\le p/4$, the spatial radial unit vector for $z\ge p/2$, and a smooth convex interpolation between them. More explicitly, choose $\zeta(t)\in[0,1]$, equal to zero for $t\le1/4$ and one for $t\ge1/2$, with $|\zeta'|\le8$, and set $$Y=(1-\zeta(z/p))\frac{(x_1,x_2,0)}p
            +\zeta(z/p)\frac{x}{r}.$$ On the axis use $Y=x/r$. The field has norm at most one and is tangential to the median plane. On the interpolation region, $|\nabla(z/p)|\le5/(4r)$ and $1/p\le\sqrt5/(2r)$. The product rule therefore gives $\lvert\operatorname{div}Y\rvert\le100/r$. For sufficiently distant support it is horizontal throughout $0\le z\le L$. In the interpolation region $p/r\ge2/\sqrt5$; the two drift bounds in [eq:density-drift](#eq:density-drift), with $k$ close to one, therefore imply <a id="eq:outward-weighted-divergence"></a>$$\label{eq:outward-weighted-divergence}
 n^{-1}\operatorname{div}(nY)\le-1$$ after increasing the fixed starting radius. There is no boundary contribution when integrating $\operatorname{div}(nY|F_1|^2)$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\int_{\Omega_+}n|Y\cdot\nabla F_1|^2
       \ge\frac14\int_{\Omega_+}n|F_1|^2.$$ Dropping the nonnegative $q$ term in [eq:delta-transform-boundary](#eq:delta-transform-boundary) and using [eq:strip-flux-bound](#eq:strip-flux-bound), [eq:strip-boundary-cost](#eq:strip-boundary-cost) proves $$\int_{\Omega_+}n\delta^2|\nabla\xi|^2
 \ge\left(\frac14-\mu^2-\frac{\pi^2}{4L^2}\right)
             \int_{\Omega_+}n\delta^2|\xi|^2.$$ The comparison of $g^2$ with $n\delta^2$ yields the coefficient <a id="eq:exterior-gap-constant"></a>$$\label{eq:exterior-gap-constant}
 \frac2{\pi^2}
 \left(\frac14-\frac1{100}-\frac{\pi^2}{400}\right)
       >0.0436>0.03.$$ This proves [eq:exterior-weighted-gap](#eq:exterior-weighted-gap). Real and imaginary parts give complex tests; the form-domain approximation described at the beginning of the section gives the general statement. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
#### The full weighted spectral gap

<a id="stmt-prop-weighted-gap"></a>
<div id="prop:weighted-gap" class="proposition">

**Proposition 16** (The gap at the second orbital). *For sufficiently large $D$ and all sufficiently small $\lambda\ge0$, every folded minimizing angle satisfies <a id="eq:weighted-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:weighted-gap}
 \frac12\int_{\mathbf R^3}g^2|\nabla\xi|^2
 \ge d\left(
      \int_{\mathbf R^3}g^2|\xi|^2
       -\frac{\left|\int_{\mathbf R^3}g^2\xi\right|^2}
                    {\int_{\mathbf R^3}g^2}\right),
 \qquad \xi\in H^1(g^2).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-weighted-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-weighted-gap</summary>

*Proof.* Put $w=g^2$. On the upper half-space, $f=h/g>0$ satisfies $$-\frac1{2w}\operatorname{div}(w\nabla f)=df.$$ For compact tests inside $\Omega_+$, the ground-state transform <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[FS08](#ref-FS08), Section 2.3, equation (2.12)] gives <a id="eq:odd-ground-transform"></a>$$\label{eq:odd-ground-transform}
 \frac12\int_{\Omega_+}w|\nabla\xi|^2
       -d\int_{\Omega_+}w|\xi|^2
 =\frac12\int_{\Omega_+}wf^2
                 |\nabla(\xi/f)|^2\ge0.$$ Odd tests have zero trace at the plane. The left side of [eq:odd-ground-transform](#eq:odd-ground-transform) is continuous in the weighted form norm. Cutoff and local zero-trace approximation therefore extend its nonnegativity to all odd functions in $H^1(w)$, proving the odd part of the desired gap.

We prove the even part by controlling possible loss of weighted norm at infinity. Let $w_0=2b^2$. The positive-orbital transform for $b$ identifies $\mathcal E_{w_0}$ with the quadratic form of $h_D-\epsilon_b$, under $\xi%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=21A6}%
  \KSOriginalMapsto\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}\sqrt2b\,\xi$. The identity first holds for compact smooth tests. Their density in $H^1(w_0)$, together with closedness of the Schrödinger form, extends it to the full weighted domain: the transformed approximants are Cauchy in the form norm, and their $L^2$ limit is $\sqrt2b\,\xi$. No global bound on $\nabla\log b$ is needed. By [prop:two-centre-spectrum](#prop:two-centre-spectrum), there is an even spectral gap $\Delta_{\mathrm{ev}}>0.2$: <a id="eq:unperturbed-even-gap"></a>$$\label{eq:unperturbed-even-gap}
 \mathcal E_{w_0}(\xi)\ge
       \Delta_{\mathrm{ev}}\int w_0|\xi|^2
       \quad\hbox{if \(\xi\) is even and }\int w_0\xi=0.$$ In particular it is strictly greater than $d_*$, and we choose $D$ so that $d_*<0.03$.

If the even assertion failed arbitrarily close to zero coupling, there would be $\lambda_j\downarrow0$, folded minimizers, and real even tests with $$\int w_j\xi_j=0,\qquad
 \int w_j|\xi_j|^2=1,\qquad
 \limsup_j\mathcal E_{w_j}(\xi_j)\le d_*,
 \quad w_j=g_{\lambda_j}^2.$$ Local uniform convergence to $w_0$, and its positive local lower bounds, give a subsequence converging weakly in local $H^1$ and strongly in local $L^2$ to an even $\xi$. Write $s=\int w_0|\xi|^2\le1$. The limit has mean zero, since $$\left|\int_{|x|>R}w_j\xi_j\right|
       \le\left(\int_{|x|>R}w_j\right)^{1/2},$$ and the tails on the right are uniformly small: $w_j\le n_{\lambda_j}$, and the latter densities are tight. No tightness of $w_j|\xi_j|^2$ is assumed.

Choose a smooth radial quadratic partition $\chi_R^2+\rho_R^2=1$, with $\chi_R=1$ on $r\le R$, $\chi_R=0$ on $r\ge2R$, and derivatives of size $O(R^{-1})$. Expanding gradients gives the weighted localization identity, as in the usual IMS formula <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[Simon83](#ref-Simon83), Section 3, Lemma 3.1]: $$\mathcal E_{w_j}(\xi_j)
 =\mathcal E_{w_j}(\chi_R\xi_j)
  +\mathcal E_{w_j}(\rho_R\xi_j)
  -\frac12\int w_j
       (|\nabla\chi_R|^2+|\nabla\rho_R|^2)|\xi_j|^2.$$ The last term is $O(R^{-2})$. For $R\ge R_0$, [prop:exterior-gap](#prop:exterior-gap), applied to both half-spaces, bounds the second term below by $0.03\|\rho_R\xi_j\|_{L^2(w_j)}^2$. Local lower semicontinuity and local strong convergence therefore give $$\liminf_j\mathcal E_{w_j}(\xi_j)
 \ge \mathcal E_{w_0}(\chi_R\xi)
      +0.03\left(1-\int w_0|\chi_R\xi|^2\right)-O(R^{-2}).$$ Local lower semicontinuity also gives finite global energy to the limit. Letting $R\to\infty$ and using [eq:unperturbed-even-gap](#eq:unperturbed-even-gap), we obtain $$d_*\ge\mathcal E_{w_0}(\xi)+0.03(1-s)
       \ge\Delta_{\mathrm{ev}}s+0.03(1-s)>d_*,$$ a contradiction for every $s\in[0,1]$. This proves the even assertion uniformly for all folded minimizers. Finally, reflection separates the even and odd parts orthogonally in the weighted norm and energy. The odd part has zero weighted mean. Combining the two inequalities, and then the real and imaginary parts, proves [eq:weighted-gap](#eq:weighted-gap). ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
#### A finite collection of sufficient tests

The following version will be used for the exact charge prescription. It avoids requiring that the selected coupling belong to an unspecified perturbation interval.

<a id="stmt-lem-quantitative-gap"></a>
<div id="lem:quantitative-gap" class="lemma">

**Lemma 17** (A quantitative local test for the gap). *Let $b,e,n_0,\theta_*$ be the zero-coupling data for a fixed $D$ whose even gap satisfies $\Delta_{\mathrm{ev}}>0.2$. Let $n$ satisfy the hypotheses of [prop:angle-minimizer](#prop:angle-minimizer), be axisymmetric, and let $\theta$ be a folded minimizer. Set $$\eta_0=10^{-60},\qquad M\ge10^4,\qquad S=100M.$$ Suppose $n$ is $C^2$ on a neighborhood of $\{r\ge M\}$, $0.99<k<1.01$, $0<d<0.001$, and the following tests hold.*

1.  *The two drift bounds of [eq:density-drift](#eq:density-drift) hold with $\eta=\eta_0$ for $r\ge M$ on their stated domains. For $p\ge M,\ 0\le z\le L$, the bounds [eq:strip-comparison-data](#eq:strip-comparison-data) hold with $\eta=\eta_0$ and $C=100$.*

2.  *Let $U,U_0$ be the strip solutions for $n,n_0$, and suppose $$\alpha=\inf_{0<z\le L}
         \frac{(2/\pi)\delta_*(M,z)-U_0(M,z)}{z}>0,
     \qquad \delta_*=\pi/2-\theta_*.$$ Uniformly on the cylinder $p=M,\ 0\le z\le L$, including all azimuths for $\theta$, one has $$|\partial_z(\theta-\theta_*)|
           +|\partial_z(U-U_0)|<\alpha/2.$$*

3.  *On $r\le2S$, the local weight comparison is $$\frac12\le\frac{g^2}{2b^2}\le2.$$*

*Then holds for tests supported in $r>S$, and the full gap [eq:weighted-gap](#eq:weighted-gap) holds.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-quantitative-gap"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-quantitative-gap</summary>

*Proof.* The strip derivative constant in [lem:strip-solution](#lem:strip-solution) is less than $10^{30}$. The drift coefficient in the strip has absolute value less than $3$, so the horizontal error in $L_nU$ is at most $4\cdot10^{30}\eta_0U$. Since $q\le2d<0.002$ and $\mu^2=0.01$, this proves [eq:strip-subsolution](#eq:strip-subsolution) for every $p\ge M$. At the inner cylinder the function $$\frac2\pi\delta-U
       -\left(\frac2\pi\delta_*-U_0\right)$$ vanishes at $z=0$ and has $z$-derivative of absolute value less than $\alpha/2$. Its reference term is at least $\alpha z$. Thus the required inner boundary ordering is strict. The same cutoff comparison proves [eq:strip-angle-comparison](#eq:strip-angle-comparison) for $p\ge M$.

For $r\ge S$, the vector field used above is horizontal throughout the strip. Its weighted divergence is bounded by $$-2k\,\frac2{\sqrt5}+\eta_0+\frac{100}{r}<-1.$$ All boundary support lies at $p>M$. The flux calculation and [eq:exterior-gap-constant](#eq:exterior-gap-constant) consequently give the exterior constant $0.03$ from radius $S$.

It remains to prove the even part of the full gap without a perturbative compactness argument. Choose a nonnegative smooth radial quadratic partition $\chi^2+\rho^2=1$ between $S$ and $2S$, with $$\frac12\bigl(|\nabla\chi|^2+|\nabla\rho|^2\bigr)
       \le\frac{10}{S^2}.$$ For an even test $\xi\in H^1(g^2)$, put $w=g^2$, $w_0=2b^2$, and choose $$c=\frac{\int w_0\chi\xi}{\int w_0\chi},
 \qquad f=\xi-c.$$ The denominator is positive and $\int w_0\chi f=0$. By the local weight comparison and [eq:unperturbed-even-gap](#eq:unperturbed-even-gap), $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathcal E_w(\chi f)
 &\ge\frac12\mathcal E_{w_0}(\chi f)
 \ge\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{ev}}}{2}
               \|\chi f\|_{L^2(w_0)}^2\\
 &\ge\frac{\Delta_{\mathrm{ev}}}{4}
               \|\chi f\|_{L^2(w)}^2
 \ge0.05\|\chi f\|_{L^2(w)}^2.
 \end{aligned}$$ The exterior estimate gives $\mathcal E_w(\rho f)\ge0.03\|\rho f\|_{L^2(w)}^2$. Although $f$ need not have compact support, it belongs to $H^1(w)$, because $\int w=2$; thus the established cutoff extension applies. Localization yields $$\mathcal E_w(\xi)=\mathcal E_w(f)
 \ge\left(0.03-\frac{10}{S^2}\right)\|f\|_{L^2(w)}^2
 \ge d\,\mathop{\mathrm{Var}}_w(\xi).$$ The last step uses $d<0.001$, the stated lower bound on $S$, and the fact that subtracting any constant gives squared norm at least the weighted variance. The odd ground-state transform [eq:odd-ground-transform](#eq:odd-ground-transform) does not require small coupling and completes the proof, including complex tests. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:ensemble"></a>
### Certification of the ensemble kinetic minimum

We now pass from the weighted spectral estimate to the full fermionic constrained search. The argument uses the density regularity from [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity), the positive orbitals and their equations from [prop:orbital-equations](#prop:orbital-equations), and [eq:weighted-gap](#eq:weighted-gap). These hypotheses will also suffice when they are established by the finite tests in [sec:parameters](#sec:parameters).

<a id="pm-h-21"></a>
#### Ensembles and the Pauli bound

An ensemble is a positive trace-class operator $\Gamma$ on $\mathfrak H_3=\bigwedge^3\mathfrak h$, where $\mathfrak h=L^2(\mathbf R^3;\mathbf C^2)$, with $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Gamma=1$. Its one-particle reduced density matrix $\gamma_\Gamma$ is characterized by <a id="eq:ensemble-rdm"></a>$$\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\gamma_\Gamma A)
 =\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\left(\Gamma\sum_{i=1}^3 A_i\right)
 \qquad\text{for every bounded operator $A$ on $\mathfrak h$}.
 \label{eq:ensemble-rdm}$$ In particular, $\gamma_\Gamma\ge0$ and $\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\gamma_\Gamma=3$. For any unit spin orbital $f\in\mathfrak h$, the number operator $\sum_i(|f\rangle\langle f|)_i$ is an orthogonal projection on $\mathfrak H_3$. Indeed, extend $f$ to an orthonormal basis of $\mathfrak h$. In the induced wedge basis, this operator has eigenvalue one precisely on the determinants that contain $f$, and zero otherwise. Its expectation is therefore at most one. Applying [eq:ensemble-rdm](#eq:ensemble-rdm) gives <a id="eq:ensemble-pauli"></a>$$0\le\gamma_\Gamma\le\mathop{\mathrm{id}},
 \qquad \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\gamma_\Gamma=3.
 \label{eq:ensemble-pauli}$$ This argument permits arbitrary complex spinors and arbitrary spin coherences.

<a id="eq:ensemble-density"></a>
<a id="eq:ensemble-search"></a>
Write the spectral decomposition over positive eigenvalues as $\gamma_\Gamma=\sum_j\nu_j|\psi_j\rangle\langle\psi_j|$. The spin-summed density and kinetic expectation are <a id="eq:ensemble-kinetic"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 n_\Gamma(x)
 &=\sum_j\nu_j|\psi_j(x)|_{\mathbf C^2}^2,
 \label{eq:ensemble-density}\\
 T[\Gamma]
 &=\frac12\sum_j\nu_j\|\nabla\psi_j\|_2^2.
 \label{eq:ensemble-kinetic}
\end{aligned}$$ The second expression takes the value $+\infty$ if necessary; for finite kinetic expectation every $\psi_j$ with $\nu_j>0$ belongs to $H^1(\mathbf R^3;\mathbf C^2)$. It equals the expectation of the three-electron kinetic-energy form, by applying [eq:ensemble-rdm](#eq:ensemble-rdm) to increasing bounded spectral truncations of $-\Delta/2$ and using monotone convergence. The ensemble constrained-search value is $$T_{\mathrm{ens}}[n]
 =\inf\{T[\Gamma]:\Gamma\ge0,\ \mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Gamma=1,\ n_\Gamma=n\}.
 \label{eq:ensemble-search}$$

Set $\widehat g=g/\sqrt2$, and let $P_g$ denote the orthogonal projection onto the normalized spatial orbital $\widehat g$. The orbitals $\widehat g$ and $h$ are orthonormal by their norms and parity. Thus <a id="eq:ensemble-determinant"></a>$$\Phi_\theta
 = (\widehat g\uparrow)\wedge(\widehat g\downarrow)\wedge(h\uparrow)
 \label{eq:ensemble-determinant}$$ is a normalized determinant, with finite kinetic energy and density $2\widehat g^2+h^2=g^2+h^2=n$.

<a id="stmt-prop-ensemble-certificate"></a>
<div id="prop:ensemble-certificate" class="proposition">

**Proposition 18** (An ensemble kinetic certificate). *Assume the density regularity of [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity), the orbital hypotheses of [prop:orbital-equations](#prop:orbital-equations), and the weighted gap [eq:weighted-gap](#eq:weighted-gap). Then the formal potential $V$ satisfies $\int_{\mathbf R^3}n|V|<\infty$. Every ensemble with density $n$ and finite kinetic energy obeys <a id="eq:ensemble-certificate"></a>$$T[\Gamma]+\int_{\mathbf R^3}Vn\ge d.
 \label{eq:ensemble-certificate}$$ The determinant $\Phi_\theta$ attains equality. Consequently, <a id="eq:ensemble-minimum"></a>$$T_{\mathrm{ens}}[n]
 =T[|\Phi_\theta\rangle\langle\Phi_\theta|]
 =d-\int_{\mathbf R^3}Vn.
 \label{eq:ensemble-minimum}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-ensemble-certificate"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-ensemble-certificate</summary>

*Proof.* We first verify the absolute integrability required to use $V$. By [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity), $\Delta n\in L^1(\mathbf R^3)$ and $\sqrt n\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)$. Local positivity and regularity give the almost-everywhere identity $$\sqrt n\,\Delta\sqrt n
 =\frac12\Delta n-|\nabla\sqrt n|^2.$$ Using the formula for $V$ in [prop:orbital-equations](#prop:orbital-equations), the finite angle energy and $\int n\sin^2\theta=1$, we obtain <a id="eq:ensemble-V-integrability"></a>$$\int n|V|
 \le \frac14\|\Delta n\|_1
      +\frac12\|\nabla\sqrt n\|_2^2
      +\frac12\int n|\nabla\theta|^2+d
 <\infty.
 \label{eq:ensemble-V-integrability}$$ The local assertion $V\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$ was proved in [prop:orbital-equations](#prop:orbital-equations).

For a scalar orbital $f$, consider the quadratic expression $$q_V[f]=\frac12\int|\nabla f|^2+\int V|f|^2,
 \qquad
 f\in\mathcal E_V
 :=\left\{f\in H^1(\mathbf R^3):\int|V||f|^2<\infty\right\}.$$ Only this expression on its indicated test class is needed; we do not assume an ordinary Hamiltonian or a closed form with potential $V$. For $f\in C_c^\infty(\mathbf R^3;\mathbf C)$, the function $f/g$ is admissible in [eq:weighted-gap](#eq:weighted-gap), since $g$ is positive and bounded below on each compact set. Testing $(-\Delta/2+V)g=0$ locally with $|f|^2/g$ gives the positive-orbital identity <a id="eq:ensemble-orbital-bound"></a>$$q_V[f]
 =\frac12\int g^2\left|\nabla\left(\frac f g\right)\right|^2
 \ge d\left(\|f\|_2^2-|\langle\widehat g,f\rangle|^2\right).
 \label{eq:ensemble-orbital-bound}$$ The identity uses real parts in the gradient expansion and therefore holds for complex $f$ as well. This is the positive ground-state representation; see <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[FS08](#ref-FS08), Section 2.3, equation (2.12)].

We record the extension of [eq:ensemble-orbital-bound](#eq:ensemble-orbital-bound) carefully. On a fixed compact set, $H^1$ approximation by smooth functions passes through the potential integral: $V\in L^2$ there and strong $H^1$ convergence implies strong $L^4$ convergence, hence convergence of the squared moduli in $L^2$. Thus the inequality holds for compactly supported $H^1$ orbitals. Choose real cutoffs $0\le\chi_R\le1$ that equal one on $|x|\le R$, vanish on $|x|\ge2R$, and satisfy $|\nabla\chi_R|\le C/R$. For $f\in\mathcal E_V$, $\chi_Rf\to f$ in $H^1$, while dominated convergence gives $\int V|\chi_Rf|^2\to\int V|f|^2$. The projection term also converges in $L^2$. This proves [eq:ensemble-orbital-bound](#eq:ensemble-orbital-bound) on all of $\mathcal E_V$.

Apply this scalar inequality to both components of a spinor $\psi$. With $\widetilde P_g=P_g\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathbf C^2}$, the resulting estimate is <a id="eq:ensemble-spinor-bound"></a>$$\frac12\|\nabla\psi\|_2^2+\int V|\psi|_{\mathbf C^2}^2
 \ge d\bigl(\|\psi\|_2^2
             -\langle\psi,\widetilde P_g\psi\rangle\bigr).
 \label{eq:ensemble-spinor-bound}$$ Now let $\Gamma$ have density $n$ and finite kinetic expectation. By [eq:ensemble-density](#eq:ensemble-density), [eq:ensemble-V-integrability](#eq:ensemble-V-integrability) and Tonelli’s theorem, $$\sum_j\nu_j\int|V||\psi_j|_{\mathbf C^2}^2=\int|V|n<\infty.$$ Hence every occupied natural spin orbital belongs componentwise to $\mathcal E_V$, and the potential terms can be summed absolutely. Summing [eq:ensemble-spinor-bound](#eq:ensemble-spinor-bound) and using [eq:ensemble-pauli](#eq:ensemble-pauli) gives $$\begin{aligned}
 T[\Gamma]+\int Vn
 &\ge d\left(\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\gamma_\Gamma
                -\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}(\gamma_\Gamma\widetilde P_g)\right)\\
 &\ge d(3-2)=d.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\widetilde P_g$ has rank two and $0\le\gamma_\Gamma\le\mathop{\mathrm{id}}$. No rank bound on $\Gamma$ is used.

Finally, the orbitals $\widehat g,h$ belong to $\mathcal E_V$ by [eq:ensemble-V-integrability](#eq:ensemble-V-integrability). If $f$ denotes either of them, with respective energy $\varepsilon=0,d$, testing its distributional equation by $\chi_R^2 f$ gives $$q_V[\chi_R f]
 =\varepsilon\|\chi_R f\|_2^2
   +\frac12\int|\nabla\chi_R|^2|f|^2.$$ All terms converge as above, so $q_V[\widehat g]=0$ and $q_V[h]=d$. The determinant in [eq:ensemble-determinant](#eq:ensemble-determinant) therefore satisfies $$T[|\Phi_\theta\rangle\langle\Phi_\theta|]+\int Vn
 =2q_V[\widehat g]+q_V[h]=d.$$ This proves the minimum over all ensembles with finite kinetic energy; ensembles with infinite kinetic expectation cannot lower it. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
#### Identification of a representing potential

<a id="stmt-prop-potential-identification"></a>
<div id="prop:potential-identification" class="proposition">

**Proposition 19** (Transfer to a representing Hamiltonian). *Assume the hypotheses of [prop:ensemble-certificate](#prop:ensemble-certificate). Let $v\in L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3)+L^\infty(\mathbf R^3)$ be real and spin independent, and let $h_v=-\Delta/2+v$ be its spatial one-particle operator, defined by the closed form on $H^1(\mathbf R^3)$. Suppose $H_v=\sum_{i=1}^3(h_v\otimes\mathop{\mathrm{id}}_{\mathbf C^2})_i$ on $\mathfrak H_3$ has a ground eigenspace supporting an ensemble $\Gamma_s$ with $n_{\Gamma_s}=n$. Then $\Phi_\theta$ belongs to that ground eigenspace, and there is a real constant $e_g$ such that <a id="eq:ensemble-potential-identification"></a>$$h_v g=e_g g,
 \qquad v=V+e_g\quad\text{almost everywhere}.
 \label{eq:ensemble-potential-identification}$$ In particular, $V\in L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3)+L^\infty(\mathbf R^3)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-potential-identification"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-potential-identification</summary>

<a id="eq:ensemble-standard-form-bound"></a>
*Proof.* The potential class supplies, for every $\varepsilon>0$, a constant $C_\varepsilon$ such that $$\int|v||f|^2
 \le\varepsilon\,\frac12\|\nabla f\|_2^2
      +C_\varepsilon\|f\|_2^2,
 \qquad f\in H^1(\mathbf R^3).
 \label{eq:ensemble-standard-form-bound}$$ Indeed, write $v=v_1+v_\infty$ and truncate $v_1$ at a sufficiently large amplitude. Its remaining $L^{3/2}$ tail has arbitrarily small norm and is controlled by the Sobolev inequality; the truncated part and $v_\infty$ are bounded. Summing [eq:ensemble-standard-form-bound](#eq:ensemble-standard-form-bound) over spins and particles gives $$q_{H_v}[\Xi]
 \ge\frac{1-\varepsilon}{2}\sum_{i=1}^3\|\nabla_i\Xi\|_2^2
       -3C_\varepsilon\|\Xi\|_2^2$$ on the many-electron $H^1$ form domain. If $E=\inf\sigma(H_v)$, every normalized vector in its ground eigenspace consequently has kinetic expectation at most $(E+3C_\varepsilon)/(1-\varepsilon)$ for any $0<\varepsilon<1$. Expanding $\Gamma_s$ in its trace-class spectral decomposition proves that $T[\Gamma_s]<\infty$, even for an infinite-dimensional ground eigenspace. The absolute potential expectation is finite by the same form bound, and $$E=T[\Gamma_s]+\int vn.$$

By [prop:ensemble-certificate](#prop:ensemble-certificate), the determinant has no larger kinetic expectation and has the same density. Thus $$E\le q_{H_v}[\Phi_\theta]
 =T[|\Phi_\theta\rangle\langle\Phi_\theta|]+\int vn
 \le T[\Gamma_s]+\int vn=E.$$ The square-root representation of the closed form <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[Kato](#ref-Kato), Chapter VI, Theorem 2.23] shows that equality at its bottom implies $(H_v-E)^{1/2}\Phi_\theta=0$. Hence $\Phi_\theta$ is itself a ground-state eigenvector.

It remains to extract a spatial eigenfunction without any assumption on the other ground states or on the multiplicities of one-particle levels. Denote the sesquilinear form of $h_v$ by $$q_v(f_1,f_2)
 =\frac12\int\nabla\overline{f_1}\cdot\nabla f_2
       +\int v\overline{f_1}f_2,$$ linear in its second argument. For $f\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)$, replace the single down-spin orbital in [eq:ensemble-determinant](#eq:ensemble-determinant) and set $$\Xi_f=(\widehat g\uparrow)\wedge(f\downarrow)\wedge(h\uparrow).$$ This vector lies in the many-electron form domain. Expanding the one-body form between these determinants, spin orthogonality and $\langle\widehat g,h\rangle=0$ give $$\begin{aligned}
 q_{H_v}(\Phi_\theta,\Xi_f)
 &=q_v(\widehat g,f)\\
 &\quad+\langle\widehat g,f\rangle
       \bigl(q_v(\widehat g,\widehat g)+q_v(h,h)\bigr).
 \end{aligned}$$ If $\langle\widehat g,f\rangle=0$, then $\Xi_f$ is orthogonal to $\Phi_\theta$, and the ground-state form equation makes the left side zero. Therefore $$q_v(\widehat g,f)=0
 \qquad\text{whenever }\langle\widehat g,f\rangle=0.$$ Put $e_g=q_v(\widehat g,\widehat g)\in\mathbf R$. Decomposing an arbitrary $f\in H^1$ into its component parallel to $\widehat g$ and its orthogonal component yields $$q_v(\widehat g,f)=e_g\langle\widehat g,f\rangle
 \qquad(f\in H^1).$$ By Hermitian symmetry this is equivalent to $q_v(f,\widehat g)=e_g\langle f,\widehat g\rangle$. The closed-form representation theorem <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[Kato](#ref-Kato), Chapter VI, Theorem 2.1(iii)] therefore gives $\widehat g\in\mathop{\mathrm{Dom}}(h_v)$ and $h_v\widehat g=e_g\widehat g$.

Subtracting $(-\Delta/2+V)g=0$ now gives $(v-V-e_g)g=0$ in distributions. The product is locally integrable: $v\in L^{3/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}$, $V\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$, and $g\in H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$ is locally bounded. Since $g$ is continuous and strictly positive, division on each compact set proves $v=V+e_g$ almost everywhere. Adding a constant preserves the stated potential class, completing the proof. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:obstruction"></a>
### The obstruction from the median plane

We now fix the density and angle; no parameter tends to zero in this section. We use the following analytic properties established above: $n>0$ is continuous, even under reflection in $z=0$, and axisymmetric, with $\int_{\mathbf R^3}n=3$. We retain the regularity $n\in H^2(\mathbf R^3)$, $\sqrt n\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)$, and $\Delta n\in L^1(\mathbf R^3)$ from [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity). The angle $\theta$ is odd, locally $C^1$, and $0<\theta<\pi/2$ on $z>0$. It has finite weighted gradient energy and satisfies <a id="eq:obstruction-angle"></a>$$\operatorname{div}(n\nabla\theta)=-d n\sin(2\theta),
 \qquad d>0.
 \label{eq:obstruction-angle}$$ The orbitals and the formal potential have the properties <a id="eq:obstruction-normalization"></a><a id="eq:obstruction-regularity"></a><a id="eq:obstruction-orbitals"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 g=\sqrt n\cos\theta>0,\qquad h=\sqrt n\sin\theta,
 \qquad \|g\|_2^2=2,\quad \|h\|_2^2=1,
 \label{eq:obstruction-normalization}\\
 g,h\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)\cap H^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R^3),\qquad
 V=\frac{\Delta g}{2g}\in L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R^3),
 \qquad \int_{\mathbf R^3}n|V|<\infty,
 \label{eq:obstruction-regularity}\\
 \left(-\frac12\Delta+V\right)g=0,\qquad
 \left(-\frac12\Delta+V\right)h=dh
 \quad\text{in distributions}.
 \label{eq:obstruction-orbitals}
\end{gathered}$$ In particular, $g$ and $V$ are even and $h$ is odd under the reflection. These are the conclusions of [prop:orbital-equations](#prop:orbital-equations), [prop:ensemble-certificate](#prop:ensemble-certificate); the arguments below use only the displayed analytic properties, rather than a small-coupling hypothesis. The density translation limits and the planar lower bound will be invoked with fixed $k>0$, $\beta\in\mathbf R$, and $A(r)=e^{-kr}r^\beta$.

Suppose provisionally that <a id="eq:admissible-formal-potential"></a>$$V\in L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3)+L^\infty(\mathbf R^3).
 \label{eq:admissible-formal-potential}$$ Then the quadratic form $$q_V[u]=\frac12\int_{\mathbf R^3}|\nabla u|^2+
              \int_{\mathbf R^3}V|u|^2,
 \qquad u\in H^1(\mathbf R^3),$$ is closed and bounded below. Indeed, truncating the $L^{3/2}$ part leaves an arbitrarily small $L^{3/2}$ norm, whose form is controlled by Sobolev’s inequality; the truncated part and the original bounded part are bounded multipliers. The same estimate shows that the distributional identities in [eq:obstruction-orbitals](#eq:obstruction-orbitals) extend, by density, to all $H^1$ form tests. Thus $g$ and $h$ are eigenfunctions of this ordinary form operator at energies $0$ and $d$, respectively.

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
#### Limits of translated potentials and orbitals

<a id="stmt-lem-translated-orbital-compactness"></a>
<div id="lem:translated-orbital-compactness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 20** (Compactness under escaping translations). *Write $V=V_{\mathrm c}+V_{\mathrm b}$, where $V_{\mathrm c}\in L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3)$ and $V_{\mathrm b}\in L^\infty(\mathbf R^3)$. If $|y_j|\to\infty$, then $$V_{\mathrm c}(y_j+\cdot)\longrightarrow0
 \quad\text{in }L^{3/2}_{\mathrm{loc}}(\mathbf R^3).$$ After passing to a subsequence there is a real bounded function $W$ such that $$V_{\mathrm b}(y_j+\cdot)\stackrel{*}{\rightharpoonup}W
 \quad\text{locally in }L^\infty,
 \qquad \|W\|_\infty\le\|V_{\mathrm b}\|_\infty.$$ Suppose, in addition, that $u_j\in H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ solves $$\left(-\frac12\Delta+V(y_j+\cdot)\right)u_j=\varepsilon u_j$$ for one fixed real $\varepsilon$, and that $u_j$ is bounded in $L^2$ on every compact set. A further subsequence converges strongly in $L^2_{\mathrm{loc}}$ and weakly in $H^1_{\mathrm{loc}}$ to a solution of $$\left(-\frac12\Delta+W\right)u=\varepsilon u.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-translated-orbital-compactness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-translated-orbital-compactness</summary>

*Proof.* Every fixed translated ball eventually lies in an arbitrarily distant tail of $V_{\mathrm c}$, proving its norm convergence. Weak-star compactness and a diagonal subsequence on expanding balls give $W$.

To prove the assertion about solutions, let $\chi$ be a smooth compact cutoff. Testing the equation with $\chi^2u_j$ and taking the real part gives the usual cutoff identity. Its possibly negative critical-potential term is bounded by $$\int |V_{\mathrm c}(y_j+x)|\,|\chi u_j|^2\,dx
 \le C_{\mathrm S}\|V_{\mathrm c}(y_j+\cdot)\|_{L^{3/2}(\mathop{\mathrm{supp}}\chi)}
          \|\nabla(\chi u_j)\|_2^2.$$ For large $j$ this is absorbed into the kinetic term. The bounded potential, the eigenvalue, and the cutoff derivative are controlled by the assumed local $L^2$ bounds. Hence the sequence is bounded in local $H^1$. Rellich compactness gives the stated convergences.

For a compact smooth test $\varphi$, the critical-potential term tends to zero by Hölder’s and Sobolev’s inequalities. The bounded-potential term passes to the limit because $u_j\varphi\to u\varphi$ in $L^1$ and the multipliers are uniformly bounded, while their pairing with the fixed function $u\varphi$ converges weak star. The gradient term passes by weak convergence. This proves the limiting equation. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-prop-potential-limits"></a>
<div id="prop:potential-limits" class="proposition">

**Proposition 21** (The unique potential limit at infinity). *Assume [eq:admissible-formal-potential](#eq:admissible-formal-potential) and the density translation limits [eq:plane-translate](#eq:plane-translate), [eq:bulk-translate](#eq:bulk-translate). For every sequence $|y_j|\to\infty$, every local weak-star subsequential limit of $V_{\mathrm b}(y_j+\cdot)$ is the constant <a id="eq:potential-limit-constant"></a>$$c_\infty=d+\frac{k^2}{2}.
 \label{eq:potential-limit-constant}$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-potential-limits"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-potential-limits</summary>

*Proof.* **Centers on the median plane.** First let $y_j=p_j\omega_j$, where $p_j\to\infty$, $\omega_j\cdot e_z=0$, $|\omega_j|=1$, and, after subselection, $\omega_j\to\omega_*$. Put $$c_j=\frac{A(p_j)}{p_j},\qquad
 g_j(x)=\frac{g(y_j+x)}{c_j},\qquad
 h_j(x)=\frac{h(y_j+x)}{c_j}.$$ By [eq:plane-translate](#eq:plane-translate), for a constant $a_*>0$, <a id="eq:plane-normalized-orbitals"></a>$$g_j^2+h_j^2=\frac{n(y_j+\cdot)}{c_j^2}
 \longrightarrow a_*^2z^2e^{-2k\omega_*\cdot x}
 \quad\text{locally uniformly}.
 \label{eq:plane-normalized-orbitals}$$ In particular these orbitals are locally bounded. Take any subsequential bounded-potential limit $W$. By [lem:translated-orbital-compactness](#lem:translated-orbital-compactness), a further subsequence has limits $g_\infty,h_\infty$ satisfying the two orbital equations with potential $W$ and energies $0,d$. Their strong local $L^2$ convergence and [eq:plane-normalized-orbitals](#eq:plane-normalized-orbitals) give $$0\le g_\infty(x)\le a_*|z|e^{-k\omega_*\cdot x},\qquad
 g_\infty^2+h_\infty^2=a_*^2z^2e^{-2k\omega_*\cdot x}
 \quad\text{a.e.}$$ Since $W$ is bounded, local elliptic regularity makes $g_\infty$ continuous. It vanishes on the interior plane $z=0$. The local Harnack inequality for a nonnegative solution with bounded zeroth-order coefficient <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[SimonPDE](#ref-SimonPDE), Lecture 18, Theorem 1] forces $g_\infty\equiv0$; no sign condition on $W$ is needed. The sign of $h$ on each half-space therefore determines $$h_\infty(x)=a_*z e^{-k\omega_*\cdot x}.$$ Because $\omega_*\cdot e_z=0$, its Laplacian is $k^2h_\infty$. The limiting equation gives $(W-d-k^2/2)h_\infty=0$. This determines $W=c_\infty$ almost everywhere; the plane where $h_\infty=0$ has zero volume.

**A positive angle bound above the plane.** We next show <a id="eq:angle-plane-parallel-lower-bound"></a>$$\inf_{x_1,x_2\in\mathbf R}\theta(x_1,x_2,1)>0.
 \label{eq:angle-plane-parallel-lower-bound}$$ On each compact subset of the open upper half-space, the normalized density in [eq:plane-normalized-orbitals](#eq:plane-normalized-orbitals) is uniformly bounded above and below by positive constants. The translated angle solves $$\operatorname{div}\!\left(\frac{n(y_j+x)}{c_j^2}
                         \nabla\theta(y_j+x)\right)
 =-d\frac{n(y_j+x)}{c_j^2}\sin\bigl(2\theta(y_j+x)\bigr).$$ On each such compact set the scalar coefficients have uniform positive lower and finite upper bounds, and the right-hand sides are uniformly bounded. Local energy estimates and the interior Hölder estimate for uniformly elliptic divergence-form equations <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[SimonPDE](#ref-SimonPDE), Lecture 18, Theorem 3] therefore give local uniform compactness of the bounded angles. These estimates require no bounds on derivatives of the normalized densities. The identity $$g_j=\sqrt{n(y_j+\cdot)/c_j^2}\cos\theta(y_j+\cdot),$$ together with $g_\infty=0$, implies that every such angle limit equals $\pi/2$. Consequently $\theta(x_1,x_2,1)\to\pi/2$ as $x_1^2+x_2^2\to\infty$. On the remaining compact part it has a strictly positive minimum, proving [eq:angle-plane-parallel-lower-bound](#eq:angle-plane-parallel-lower-bound).

Choose $m\in(0,\pi/2)$ below this infimum. In $z>1$, the angle is a supersolution of $-\operatorname{div}(n\nabla\theta)\ge0$. For $w=(m-\theta)_+$, whose trace on $z=1$ is zero, test with $\chi_R^2w$, where $\chi_R$ is a spatial cutoff with $|\nabla\chi_R|\le C/R$. The resulting estimate is $$\int_{z>1} n\chi_R^2|\nabla w|^2
 \le4\int_{z>1}n w^2|\nabla\chi_R|^2\longrightarrow0.$$ Here $w$ is bounded and $n$ is integrable. Thus $w$ is constant on this connected half-space, and its zero trace makes it zero. We have proved $\theta\ge m$ throughout $z\ge1$.

**Centers whose heights diverge.** Set $\delta=\pi/2-\theta$ on the upper half-space. On $z\ge1$, $$0\le\delta\le\frac\pi2-m,\qquad
 \operatorname{div}(n\nabla\delta)=d n\sin(2\delta),\qquad
 d\sin(2\delta)\ge c_0\delta$$ for some $c_0>0$. The last assertion follows by minimizing the continuous positive function $d\sin(2s)/s$ on $[0,\pi/2-m]$, with its value at zero defined as $2d$.

Now let $(y_j)_3\to+\infty$, and pass to a subsequence with $y_j/|y_j|\to\omega_*$. By [eq:bulk-translate](#eq:bulk-translate), $$N_j(x)=\frac{n(y_j+x)}{n(y_j)}
 \longrightarrow N_\infty(x)=e^{-2k\omega_*\cdot x}
 \quad\text{locally uniformly}.$$ The same local energy and Hölder estimates give, along a subsequence, local uniform and weak local $H^1$ convergence of $\delta(y_j+\cdot)$ to a bounded nonnegative function $w$. Passing the divergence equation to the limit requires only the displayed uniform convergence of the coefficients, not convergence of their derivatives. We obtain $$\operatorname{div}(N_\infty\nabla w)=dN_\infty\sin(2w),\qquad
 (\Delta-2k\omega_*\cdot\nabla)w=d\sin(2w)\ge c_0w.$$ Local elliptic regularity makes $w$ smooth. A short maximum-principle argument shows that $w=0$. Indeed, for $\varepsilon>0$ subtract $\varepsilon\rho(x)$, where $\rho(x)=\sqrt{1+|x|^2}$. The boundedness of $w$ implies that $w-\varepsilon\rho$ attains a global maximum at some $x_\varepsilon$. At that point, $$c_0w(x_\varepsilon)
 \le (\Delta-2k\omega_*\cdot\nabla)w(x_\varepsilon)
 \le\varepsilon(3+2k),$$ since $|\nabla\rho|\le1$ and $\Delta\rho\le3$. Comparing the maximum with any fixed point $x_0$ gives $w(x_0)\le\varepsilon\rho(x_0)+\varepsilon(3+2k)/c_0$. Letting $\varepsilon\downarrow0$ proves the assertion.

It follows that $\delta(y_j+\cdot)\to0$ locally uniformly along every such sequence. Hence $$\frac{h(y_j+x)}{\sqrt{n(y_j)}}
 =\sqrt{N_j(x)}\cos\delta(y_j+x)
 \longrightarrow e^{-k\omega_*\cdot x}
 \quad\text{locally uniformly}.$$ Apply [lem:translated-orbital-compactness](#lem:translated-orbital-compactness) to this normalized orbital along any subsequence on which the bounded potentials converge. Its limiting eigenfunction equation again identifies $W=d+k^2/2$, since the limiting exponential is everywhere positive. Reflection treats centers with heights tending to $-\infty$. Let $\mathcal R$ denote reflection in the median plane. Since $V$ is even, its arbitrary decomposition satisfies $$V_{\mathrm b}(\mathcal R x)-V_{\mathrm b}(x)
 =V_{\mathrm c}(x)-V_{\mathrm c}(\mathcal R x).$$ Escaping translates of the right side tend to zero in local $L^{3/2}$. The left side is uniformly bounded, so its local weak-star limits are also zero. Reflecting the center and local coordinates therefore gives the same constant limit for negative heights.

**All remaining sequences.** Given any escaping sequence, its heights have a subsequence that tends to $+\infty$, tends to $-\infty$, or converges to a finite number. The first two cases were just proved. In the third, project the centers onto the median plane. Their horizontal radii tend to infinity, and the original centers differ from these plane centers by convergent bounded displacements. The plane-centered conclusion therefore applies. To justify the moving displacement for weak-star convergence, translate each fixed compactly supported $L^1$ test by the negative displacement; these tests converge in $L^1$, while the multipliers stay uniformly bounded. Thus their limit remains the same constant. These cases exhaust every subsequential bounded-potential limit. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
#### From translated limits to an exterior form bound

<a id="stmt-lem-exterior-form-limit"></a>
<div id="lem:exterior-form-limit" class="lemma">

**Lemma 22** (Uniform exterior form convergence). *Let $V=V_{\mathrm c}+V_{\mathrm b}$ be as in [lem:translated-orbital-compactness](#lem:translated-orbital-compactness). Suppose every bounded-potential limit along escaping translates is a fixed constant $c_\infty$. For every $\varepsilon>0$ there is $R_\varepsilon<\infty$ such that <a id="eq:exterior-form-coercivity"></a>$$q_V[u]\ge
 \left(\frac12-\varepsilon\right)\|\nabla u\|_2^2
 +(c_\infty-\varepsilon)\|u\|_2^2
 \label{eq:exterior-form-coercivity}$$ for every $u\in H^1(\mathbf R^3)$ vanishing almost everywhere on $\{|x|<R_\varepsilon\}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-exterior-form-limit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-exterior-form-limit</summary>

*Proof.* Fix a ball $B_L$. We first claim <a id="eq:local-form-uniformity"></a>$$\sup_{\substack{u\in H^1_0(B_L)\\\|u\|_{H^1(B_L)}\le1}}
 \left|\int_{B_L}\bigl(V(y+x)-c_\infty\bigr)|u(x)|^2\,dx\right|
 \longrightarrow0\qquad (|y|\to\infty).
 \label{eq:local-form-uniformity}$$ Sobolev’s inequality and the vanishing local $L^{3/2}$ norm prove the claim for the critical part. If the bounded part failed, choose escaping centers and tests violating it. Extract a weak-star subsequence of the bounded multipliers and a subsequence of the tests converging strongly in $L^2(B_L)$. Their squares converge in $L^1$, because $$\bigl\||u_j|^2-|u|^2\bigr\|_1
 \le(\|u_j\|_2+\|u\|_2)\|u_j-u\|_2.$$ Uniform boundedness of the multipliers handles this changing test, and their weak-star limit is $c_\infty$. This contradicts the selected violation and proves [eq:local-form-uniformity](#eq:local-form-uniformity).

Choose once and for all a smooth, locally finite quadratic partition $\sum_\ell\chi_\ell^2=1$ with uniformly bounded overlap, each $\chi_\ell$ supported in a ball of one fixed radius, and $\sum_\ell|\nabla\chi_\ell|^2\le K$. For any prescribed $\eta>0$, [eq:local-form-uniformity](#eq:local-form-uniformity) applies with error $\eta$ to all these balls whose centers are sufficiently distant. If $u$ is supported sufficiently far out, every nonzero $\chi_\ell u$ has such a center. Summing the local potential estimates gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \left|\int(V-c_\infty)|u|^2\right|
 &\le\eta\sum_\ell\|\chi_\ell u\|_{H^1}^2\\
 &\le\eta\|\nabla u\|_2^2+\eta(1+K)\|u\|_2^2.
\end{aligned}$$ Here the cross terms cancel because $\sum_\ell\chi_\ell\nabla\chi_\ell=0$. Choosing $\eta\le\varepsilon/(1+K)$ proves [eq:exterior-form-coercivity](#eq:exterior-form-coercivity). This argument uses a uniform local form estimate, not a pointwise limit of $V$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-26"></a>
#### The weighted trace contradiction

<a id="stmt-thm-obstruction"></a>
<div id="thm:obstruction" class="theorem">

**Theorem 23** (A fixed-density obstruction). *Let $n,\theta,g,h,V,d$ have the analytic properties stated at the beginning of this section, with $d>0$. Suppose that [eq:plane-tail](#eq:plane-tail), [eq:plane-translate](#eq:plane-translate), [eq:bulk-translate](#eq:bulk-translate) hold with fixed $k>0$ and $\beta$, and that the weighted gap [eq:weighted-gap](#eq:weighted-gap) holds. Then $$V%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=2209}%
  \KSOriginalNotin\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3)+L^\infty(\mathbf R^3).$$ Moreover, $n$ is not the density of a ground-state ensemble of any three-electron noninteracting Hamiltonian with a single real, spin-independent potential in this class. These hypotheses are all fixed-parameter hypotheses; no perturbative neighborhood is required.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-obstruction"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-obstruction</summary>

*Proof.* Assume first that [eq:admissible-formal-potential](#eq:admissible-formal-potential) holds. [prop:potential-limits](#prop:potential-limits), [lem:exterior-form-limit](#lem:exterior-form-limit) give [eq:exterior-form-coercivity](#eq:exterior-form-coercivity) with $c_\infty=d+k^2/2$. Since $d>0$, choose <a id="eq:agmon-exponent-choice"></a>$$k<a<\sqrt{k^2+2d},\qquad
 0<\varepsilon<\min\left\{\frac12,
                       c_\infty-\frac{a^2}{2}\right\}.
 \label{eq:agmon-exponent-choice}$$ We use the exponential-weight method <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[Agmon](#ref-Agmon)], spelling out the estimate and its $H^1$ conclusion for the form bound obtained here.

Let $\eta$ be a smooth cutoff which vanishes on the ball required by [eq:exterior-form-coercivity](#eq:exterior-form-coercivity) and equals one outside a slightly larger ball. With $\rho(x)=\sqrt{1+|x|^2}$, put $$f_T(x)=a\min\{\rho(x),T\},\qquad
 u_T=\eta e^{f_T}g.$$ The function $f_T$ is bounded and Lipschitz with $|\nabla f_T|\le a$. Thus $u_T\in H^1$, and the weak eigenfunction equation for $g$ may be tested with $\eta^2e^{2f_T}g$. Expanding the gradient gives the exact identity <a id="eq:agmon-cutoff-identity"></a>$$q_V[u_T]=\frac12\int g^2
                    \bigl|\nabla(\eta e^{f_T})\bigr|^2.
 \label{eq:agmon-cutoff-identity}$$ The part with $\eta^2|\nabla f_T|^2$ is at most $a^2\|u_T\|_2^2$. All terms involving $\nabla\eta$, including the cross term, are supported in a fixed compact annulus, where $f_T\le a\rho$ independently of $T$. Consequently $$q_V[u_T]\le\frac{a^2}{2}\|u_T\|_2^2+C$$ with $C$ independent of $T$. Combining this with the exterior form bound yields $$\left(\frac12-\varepsilon\right)\|\nabla u_T\|_2^2+
 \left(c_\infty-\varepsilon-\frac{a^2}{2}\right)\|u_T\|_2^2\le C.$$ Both coefficients are positive by [eq:agmon-exponent-choice](#eq:agmon-exponent-choice). Monotone convergence of the squared weighted functions gives the $L^2$ bound as $T\to\infty$, and weak $H^1$ compactness identifies the limit as $\eta e^{a\rho}g$. Adding back the compact interior part proves <a id="eq:weighted-ground-orbital"></a>$$e^{a\rho}g\in H^1(\mathbf R^3),\qquad
 e^{a|x|}g\in H^1(\mathbf R^3).
 \label{eq:weighted-ground-orbital}$$ The second assertion also follows by multiplying the first function by the bounded Lipschitz factor $e^{a(|x|-\rho(x))}$.

The trace map from $H^1(\mathbf R^3)$ to $L^2(\mathbf R^2)$ is bounded. The local continuity of $g$, $n$, and $\theta$, together with the zero angle trace, identifies the trace on $z=0$ as $$g(x_1,x_2,0)=\sqrt{n(x_1,x_2,0)}.$$ Thus [eq:weighted-ground-orbital](#eq:weighted-ground-orbital) implies $\int_{z=0}e^{2ap}n(p,0)<\infty$. On the other hand, [eq:plane-tail](#eq:plane-tail) gives a fixed $c_*>0$ and $p_*<\infty$ such that $$\begin{aligned}
 \int_{z=0}e^{2ap}n(p,0)\,dx_1dx_2
 &\ge2\pi c_*^2\int_{p_*}^\infty
            e^{2(a-k)p}p^{2\beta-7}\,dp\\
 &=\infty,
\end{aligned}$$ because $a>k$. This contradicts the trace bound and proves that [eq:admissible-formal-potential](#eq:admissible-formal-potential) is impossible.

Finally, the weighted gap and the analytic properties above give the ensemble kinetic certificate of [prop:ensemble-certificate](#prop:ensemble-certificate). If an admissible potential represented $n$ by a ground-state ensemble, [prop:potential-identification](#prop:potential-identification) would identify it with $V$ up to a constant. The sum space is invariant under adding constants, so this would imply [eq:admissible-formal-potential](#eq:admissible-formal-potential), already contradicted. ◻

</details>

<a id="stmt-thm-qualitative-counterexample"></a>
<div id="thm:qualitative-counterexample" class="theorem">

**Theorem 24** (A family of finite Coulombic counterexamples). *For every sufficiently large fixed $D$, there is an integer $Z_0(D)$ such that, for every integer $Z\ge Z_0(D)$, the three-electron Coulomb Hamiltonian with nuclear charges $Z,Z$ at $(0,0,\pm D/Z)$ has an absolute ground state whose density is not representable by a ground-state ensemble of a single real, spin-independent noninteracting potential in $L^{3/2}(\mathbf R^3)+L^\infty(\mathbf R^3)$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-thm-qualitative-counterexample"><summary>Proof of stmt-thm-qualitative-counterexample</summary>

*Proof.* First choose $D$ large enough for [prop:two-centre-spectrum](#prop:two-centre-spectrum), [prop:density-properties](#prop:density-properties), [prop:exterior-gap](#prop:exterior-gap). For this fixed $D$, take $\lambda>0$ sufficiently small that [prop:small-coupling](#prop:small-coupling), [prop:angle-convergence](#prop:angle-convergence), [prop:weighted-gap](#prop:weighted-gap) apply as well. The resulting three-electron state is an absolute bound ground state, and its density and angle satisfy every hypothesis of [thm:obstruction](#thm:obstruction). The planar coefficient is nonzero because the coupling is positive. All these properties hold throughout some interval $0<\lambda<\lambda_0(D)$. Choose the integer $Z_0(D)$ so that $1/Z_0(D)<\lambda_0(D)$, set $\lambda=1/Z$, and apply the scaling equivalence of [prop:scaling](#prop:scaling). ◻

</details>

The parameters in [thm:qualitative-counterexample](#thm:qualitative-counterexample) are finite but are still selected by sufficient-size conditions. The next section specifies one charge by an exact spectral and variational formula and checks its hypotheses directly using the fixed-density form of [thm:obstruction](#thm:obstruction).

<a id="sec:parameters"></a>
### An exact choice of the charges and nuclear positions

We now specify one member of the family without leaving either large parameter free. The resulting charge is an exact integer defined through spectral and variational quantities. The definition is nonnumerical: no evaluated decimal value or effective numerical upper bound for this integer is asserted.

Throughout this section fix <a id="eq:fixed-parameter-numbers"></a>$$D=10^{200},\qquad \eta_0=10^{-60},\qquad
 L=10,\qquad \mu=\frac1{10}.
 \label{eq:fixed-parameter-numbers}$$ The orbitals $b,e$, the numbers $\epsilon_b,\epsilon_e,\gamma,k_0$, and the reference angle $$\theta_*=\arctan\frac{e}{\sqrt2\,b},\qquad
 \delta_*=\frac\pi2-\theta_*\quad(z\ge0)$$ always refer to this fixed two-centre problem at zero coupling. For auxiliary couplings $t\in[0,1]$, use the operators $H_{m,t}$ of [eq:scaled-coulomb-hamiltonian](#eq:scaled-coulomb-hamiltonian). Subscripts $t$ on all other quantities refer to these auxiliary operators.

<a id="stmt-def-regular-data"></a>
<div id="def:regular-data" class="definition">

**Definition 25** (Regular auxiliary data). An auxiliary coupling $t\in[0,1]$ is called regular if all the following conditions hold.

1.  The two-electron ground level is isolated and simple, and its ground state is an axisymmetric, reflection-even spin singlet. The three-electron ground level in $S_z=1/2$ is isolated and simple, is an absolute ground level, and has an axisymmetric, reflection-odd ground state.

2.  The spin-summed density $n_t$ is everywhere positive, and <a id="eq:regular-energy-tests"></a>$$2\epsilon_b-E_3(t)>0.49,\qquad 0.99<k_t<\gamma,
     \qquad k_t=\sqrt{2(E_2(t)-E_3(t))}.
     \label{eq:regular-energy-tests}$$

3.  With the ionic ground projection $P_t$ in the distinguished-electron fiber space, and with $$F_{{\rm true},t}(x)=\sqrt3\,\Psi_t(x,\cdot),\qquad
     A_t(r)=e^{-k_t r}r^{(2-2t)/k_t-1},$$ the direct-removal amplitude satisfies <a id="eq:regular-amplitude-test"></a>$$\inf_{\omega_z>0}\ \liminf_{r\to\infty}
     \frac{\|P_tF_{{\rm true},t}(r\omega)\|}
          {\omega_z A_t(r)}>0 .
     \label{eq:regular-amplitude-test}$$

If any quantity required for these tests is undefined, the coupling is declared nonregular.

</div>

The simple levels make the densities and projected norms independent of the phases chosen for their state vectors. The Hamiltonians commute with complex conjugation, so their simple eigenvectors may be chosen real and normalized. For every regular $t$, [lem:density-regularity](#lem:density-regularity) gives the Sobolev regularity needed below. For $t>0$, smoothness away from the nuclei follows from [prop:fixed-parameter-channels](#prop:fixed-parameter-channels); at $t=0$, it follows from $n_0=2b^2+e^2$ and the scalar orbital equations for $b,e$. In particular, the exterior density has the derivatives occurring in the next definition.

<a id="pm-h-28"></a>
#### The radius and its strict comparison margin

For an integer $j>10D$ and regular $t$, define $m_t(p,z)=e^{2k_t p}n_t(p,z)$, and let $H_t(j)$ be the maximum of the following four numbers: <a id="eq:parameter-H"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 &\frac1{\eta_0}
   \sup_{|x|\ge j}
    \left|\partial_r\log n_t(x)+2k_t\right|,
 \notag\\
 &\frac1{\eta_0}
   \sup_{\substack{|x|\ge j\\0\le z\le p/2}}
    \left|\partial_p\log n_t(x)+2k_t p/r\right|,
 \notag\\
 &\frac1{\eta_0}
   \sup_{\substack{p\ge j\\0\le z\le L}}
    \frac{|\partial_pm_t(p,z)|+|\partial_p^2m_t(p,z)|}
         {m_t(p,z)},
 \notag\\
 &\frac1{100}
   \sup_{\substack{p\ge j\\0\le z_1\le z_2\le L}}
    \frac{n_t(p,z_1)}{n_t(p,z_2)}.
 \label{eq:parameter-H}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $\partial_p$ holds $z$ fixed. For nonregular $t$, set $H_t(j)=+\infty$.

Let $M$ be the smallest integer in the following set: <a id="eq:parameter-radius"></a>$$\left\{
 \begin{aligned}
 j\in\mathbf N:\;&j>10D,\quad
 \max\left\{H_0(j),\,\limsup_{t\downarrow0}H_t(j)\right\}<1,\\
 &\sup_{0\le z\le L}
 \frac{\partial_z\bigl(n_0(j,z)\partial_z\delta_*(j,z)\bigr)}
      {n_0(j,z)\delta_*(j,z)}
 <\mu^2
 \end{aligned}
 \right\}.
 \label{eq:parameter-radius}$$ The coordinates $(j,z)$ are cylindrical coordinates. For regular $t$, let $U_t(p,z)$ be the strip solution <a id="eq:parameter-strip-solution"></a>$$\frac1{n_t(p,z)}
 \partial_z\bigl(n_t(p,z)\partial_zU_t(p,z)\bigr)
 =\mu^2U_t(p,z),\qquad
 U_t(p,0)=1,\quad U_t(p,L)=0 .
 \label{eq:parameter-strip-solution}$$ It is the normalized positive solution constructed in [lem:strip-solution](#lem:strip-solution). Once $M$ is defined, put <a id="eq:parameter-alpha"></a>$$S=100M,\qquad
 \alpha=\inf_{0<z\le L}
 \frac{(2/\pi)\delta_*(M,z)-U_0(M,z)}{z}.
 \label{eq:parameter-alpha}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-parameter-radius"></a>
<div id="lem:parameter-radius" class="lemma">

**Lemma 26** (Finiteness of the radius and positivity of the margin). *The set in [eq:parameter-radius](#eq:parameter-radius) is nonempty. Consequently $M$ is a finite integer, and the number $\alpha$ in [eq:parameter-alpha](#eq:parameter-alpha) is strictly positive.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-parameter-radius"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-parameter-radius</summary>

*Proof.* First, all sufficiently small $t\ge0$ are regular. The ground-level and symmetry conditions follow from [prop:small-coupling](#prop:small-coupling). At zero coupling, $$2\epsilon_b-E_3(0)=-\epsilon_e=\frac{k_0^2}{2}>0.49,
 \qquad 0.99<k_0<\gamma,$$ where [prop:two-centre-spectrum](#prop:two-centre-spectrum) and the fixed value of $D$ give the strict numerical inequalities. They persist for small $t$. Positivity of the density and the direct-amplitude bound are supplied by the uniform channel construction in [prop:density-properties](#prop:density-properties), [lem:channel-expansion](#lem:channel-expansion).

We record why the fixed numbers in [eq:fixed-parameter-numbers](#eq:fixed-parameter-numbers) are sufficient for the uniform exterior tests. Let $\delta_0=\gamma-k_0>0$. The estimate [eq:quantitative-density-drift](#eq:quantitative-density-drift) supplies one remainder $\rho_D(R)\downarrow0$, independent of all sufficiently small $t$, for the first three unnormalized expressions in [eq:parameter-H](#eq:parameter-H). By [eq:two-centre-numerics](#eq:two-centre-numerics), <a id="eq:parameter-drift-slack"></a>$$\delta_0<\frac{200}{D},\qquad
 100(\delta_0+\delta_0^2)<10^{-195}\ll\eta_0 .
 \label{eq:parameter-drift-slack}$$ For the last line of [eq:parameter-H](#eq:parameter-H), the uniform eventual vertical ratio is at most $20$, by [eq:strip-data](#eq:strip-data). Consequently, for all sufficiently large fixed integers $j$, $$\begin{split}
 &\max\left\{H_0(j),\,\limsup_{t\downarrow0}H_t(j)\right\}\\
 &\qquad\le
 \max\left\{
   \frac{100(\delta_0+\delta_0^2)+\rho_D(j)}{\eta_0},
   \frac15\right\}<1.
 \end{split}$$ The common remainder is obtained with $D$ fixed, and the finite radius $j$ is then chosen before taking the small-coupling limsup. This uses uniform exterior estimates throughout.

We next verify the last condition in [eq:parameter-radius](#eq:parameter-radius). The scalar exterior expansions for $b,e$, including the simple odd factor of $e$, imply that, uniformly for $0<z\le L$, $$\partial_p\log(b/e)=-\delta_0+o(1),\qquad
 \partial_p^2\log(b/e)=o(1)\qquad(p\to\infty).$$ The apparent singularity $-\log z$ has no $p$-derivative. For $q>0$, both $$\frac{q}{(1+q^2)\arctan q},
 \qquad
 \frac{q(1-q^2)}{(1+q^2)^2\arctan q}$$ have absolute value at most $1$; this follows from $q/(1+q^2)\le\arctan q$. Applying these bounds to $\delta_*=\arctan(\sqrt2 b/e)$ therefore gives $$\frac{|(\delta_*)_p|}{\delta_*}\le\delta_0+o(1),\qquad
 \frac{|(\delta_*)_{pp}|}{\delta_*}\le\delta_0^2+o(1).$$ The bounds extend to $z=0$ by continuity. The cylindrical drift coefficient satisfies $\lvert p^{-1}+\partial_p\log n_0\rvert\le3$ for sufficiently large $p$. Since $L_{n_0}\delta_*=q_*\delta_*$ with $0\le q_*\le2d_*$, it follows that $$\frac{\partial_z(n_0\partial_z\delta_*)}{n_0\delta_*}
 \le 2d_*+3\delta_0+\delta_0^2+o(1)<\mu^2$$ uniformly in the strip, once $p$ is sufficiently large. The last strict inequality uses $d_*<100/D$ and [eq:parameter-drift-slack](#eq:parameter-drift-slack). Thus the defining set for $M$ is nonempty.

On the segment $p=M$, let $$f(z)=\frac2\pi\delta_*(M,z),\qquad
 v(z)=f(z)-U_0(M,z).$$ The defining strict inequality for $M$ gives $$\frac1{n_0(M,z)}
 \partial_z\bigl(n_0(M,z)\partial_zv\bigr)-\mu^2v<0,
 \qquad
 v(0)=0,\quad v(L)>0.$$ One-dimensional comparison and its boundary point conclusion give $v(z)>0$ for $0<z\le L$ and $v'(0)>0$. The quotient $v(z)/z$ consequently extends to a continuous strictly positive function on the compact segment $[0,L]$. Its minimum is positive, proving $\alpha>0$. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-29"></a>
#### The bad set and the charge

For a regular $t$, every folded minimizing angle for $n_t$ has a well-defined multiplier by [prop:angle-minimizer](#prop:angle-minimizer). In the following tests, the minimizing angle is not assumed to be axisymmetric. A supremum on the cylinder $p=M$ includes all azimuthal angles.

Define a subset $\mathcal B\subset(0,1]$ as follows. It contains every nonregular $t$, every regular $t$ with $H_t(M)\ge1$, and every remaining $t$ for which *there exists a folded minimizing angle* $\theta$, with multiplier $d$, that fails at least one of the tests <a id="eq:parameter-angle-tests"></a>$$\begin{gathered}
 0<d<0.001,\qquad
 \frac12\le\frac{n_t\cos^2\theta}{2b^2}\le2
       \quad\text{on } |x|\le2S,\\
 \sup_{\substack{p=M\\0\le z\le L}}
 \left(
   |\partial_z(\theta-\theta_*)|
   +|\partial_z(U_t-U_0)|
 \right)<\frac{\alpha}{2}.
 \end{gathered}
 \label{eq:parameter-angle-tests}$$ Thus $t%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=2209}%
  \KSOriginalNotin\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}\mathcal B$ means that the tests hold for *every* folded minimizing angle.

<a id="stmt-def-explicit-charge"></a>
<div id="def:explicit-charge" class="definition">

**Definition 27** (The exact integer charge). Using only the preceding data for the fixed unit-charge problem, define <a id="eq:explicit-charge"></a>$$Z=2+\left\lceil
       \sup_{0<t\le1}\frac1t\,\mathbf1_{\mathcal B}(t)
      \right\rceil .
 \label{eq:explicit-charge}$$

</div>

<a id="stmt-lem-explicit-charge-finite"></a>
<div id="lem:explicit-charge-finite" class="lemma">

**Lemma 28** (The charge is finite and passes all tests). *The supremum in [eq:explicit-charge](#eq:explicit-charge) is finite. The resulting $Z$ is a positive integer, and $t_*=1/Z$ is regular and lies outside $\mathcal B$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-explicit-charge-finite"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-explicit-charge-finite</summary>

*Proof.* By [lem:parameter-radius](#lem:parameter-radius), small couplings are regular and the fixed-radius condition $H_t(M)<1$ holds throughout some interval $0<t<\tau_1$. We must check the tests for all minimizers, not merely for a selected branch.

The convergence in [prop:angle-convergence](#prop:angle-convergence) holds for every sequence of folded minimizers as $t\downarrow0$: their angles converge locally uniformly to $\theta_*$, their multipliers to $d_*$, and their derivatives converge uniformly on fixed exterior compact sets. The same statements are therefore uniform over the collection of folded minimizers for sufficiently small $t$; otherwise a sequence violating uniform convergence would contradict that proposition. Since $0<d_*<100/D<0.001$, the multiplier test passes for all minimizers.

The compact set $\{|x|\le2S\}$ is now fixed. Its reference weight $2b^2$ is bounded below by a positive constant. Density convergence and angle convergence give $$\frac{n_t\cos^2\theta}{2b^2}\longrightarrow1$$ uniformly there, for all folded minimizers. This proves the second test. On the fixed cylinder $p=M$, density convergence also gives $U_t(M,\cdot)\to U_0(M,\cdot)$ in $C^1([0,L])$. For example, this follows directly from the positive integral series in [lem:strip-solution](#lem:strip-solution): $n_t$ converges uniformly to a positive function on this compact segment, and the series and its final-flux formula converge uniformly. Together with the $C^1$ angle convergence and $\alpha>0$, this proves the last test.

Consequently $\mathcal B\cap(0,\tau)=\varnothing$ for some $\tau>0$. If $$C_{\mathcal B}
 =\sup_{0<t\le1}\frac1t\,\mathbf1_{\mathcal B}(t),$$ then $0\le C_{\mathcal B}\le1/\tau$; in the empty-bad-set case the supremum is zero. Thus $Z=2+\lceil C_{\mathcal B}\rceil$ is finite and $Z>C_{\mathcal B}$. If $t_*=1/Z$ belonged to $\mathcal B$, its contribution to the supremum would be $Z$, a contradiction. Hence $t_*%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=2209}%
  \KSOriginalNotin\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}\mathcal B$, as claimed. ◻

</details>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-explicit-charge-finite-2"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-explicit-charge-finite</summary>

*Proof of [thm:main](#thm:main).* Work first with the scaled system at $t_*=1/Z>0$. By [lem:explicit-charge-finite](#lem:explicit-charge-finite), its regularity gives the specified normalizable ground state in $S_z=1/2$, and its energy is an absolute ground energy.

It is useful to check explicitly that the fixed-parameter channel theorem applies here. We know that the selected coupling passes the tests; we do not need to know that it belongs to the particular perturbation interval used earlier. Its ionic isolation supplies a positive $Q$-gap. The inequality $2\epsilon_b-E_3(t_*)>0.49$ permits a full-configuration exponential weight with exponent $a=0.98$, since $a^2/2=0.4802<0.49$. On the cutoff region $|y|\ge r/3$, the full radius is at least $\sqrt{10/9}\,r$; hence, with room for the derivative estimates, an exterior error exponent $\sigma=1.02>\gamma$ is available. Here $\gamma<1.01$ follows from [eq:two-centre-numerics](#eq:two-centre-numerics). Moreover $k_{t_*}<\gamma$ makes the retained unperturbed fast term exponentially smaller than $A_{t_*}$ times any fixed inverse power. Finally, [eq:regular-amplitude-test](#eq:regular-amplitude-test) supplies the nonzero equatorial slope in the scalar channel coefficient. The complementary plane coefficient is nonzero because $$Q(y_{1z}+y_{2z})\phi=(y_{1z}+y_{2z})\phi%
  \BeginAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct,method=hex,unicode,ActualText=2260}%
  \KSOriginalNe\EndAccSupp{pdfliteral=direct}0,$$ by even ionic parity and the fact that a nonzero $L^2$ vector cannot be supported on the hyperplane $y_{1z}+y_{2z}=0$. The injective reduced inverse preserves nonzeroness. These are exactly the fixed-parameter hypotheses of [prop:fixed-parameter-channels](#prop:fixed-parameter-channels). That proposition gives the plane lower tail and both density translation limits at $t_*$.

Choose any folded minimizing angle $\theta$ for $n_{t_*}$. All tests in [eq:parameter-angle-tests](#eq:parameter-angle-tests) hold for it, and $H_{t_*}(M)<1$ supplies the drift and strip bounds with $\eta_0=10^{-60}$ and vertical ratio at most $100$.
